|f(\Phi^{-1}(y))-f(y)|\lesssim\frac{1}{t},\quad\left|\frac{1}{\det J_{\Phi}(\Phi^{-1}(y))}-1\right|\lesssim\frac{1}{t}.

Thus,

\left|\int_{Y_{A}}\frac{f(\Phi^{-1}(y))}{y_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{dy}{|\det J_{\Phi}(\Phi^{-1}(y))|}-\int_{y_{1}>0}\frac{f(y)}{y_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}}dy\right|\lesssim\frac{1}{t},

which completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Estimate for
V_{1}
.

We observe by the change of variable
x=x^{\prime}-\eta{\mathbf{e}_{1}}
that

V_{1}(t,t+\eta)=3V(t,t+\eta)=\frac{3}{4\pi^{2}t^{3}}\int_{|x-t{\mathbf{e}_{1}}|<t}\frac{v_{0}(x+\eta{\mathbf{e}_{1}})}{(t^{2}-|x-t{\mathbf{e}_{1}}|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}}dx

and so

\partial_{s}V_{1}(t,t+\eta)=\frac{3}{4\pi^{2}t^{3}}\Omega[\partial_{x_{1}}v_{0}(\cdot+\eta\mathbf{e}_{1})](t)

For
\eta\in[0,1]
, the support of the function
x\mapsto\partial_{x_{1}}v_{0}(x+\eta{\mathbf{e}_{1}})
is included in
B_{A+1}(0)
and thus
by (
3.28
), we have
|\partial_{s}V_{1}(t,t+\eta)|\lesssim t^{-\frac{7}{2}}
for
t
large.

Estimate for
V_{2}
.

By direct computation using the expression of
V
in (
3.17
), we have

V_{2}(t,s)=5t\partial_{t}V(t,s)=\frac{5t}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{|y|<1}\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{4}y_{j}\partial_{x_{j}}v_{0}(s{\mathbf{e}_{1}}+ty)}{(1-|y|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}}dy.

By the change of variable
y=\frac{x}{t}-{\mathbf{e}_{1}}
,
we rewrite

\displaystyle V_{2}(t,t+\eta)

\displaystyle=\frac{5}{4\pi^{2}t^{2}}\int_{|x-t{\mathbf{e}_{1}}|<t}\frac{\left(\frac{x_{1}}{t}-1\right)\partial_{x_{1}}v_{0}(x+\eta{\mathbf{e}_{1}})}{(t^{2}-|x-t{\mathbf{e}_{1}}|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}}dx

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{5}{4\pi^{2}t^{3}}\int_{|x-t{\mathbf{e}_{1}}|<t}\frac{\sum_{j=2}^{4}x_{j}\partial_{x_{j}}v_{0}(x+\eta{\mathbf{e}_{1}})}{(t^{2}-|x-t{\mathbf{e}_{1}}|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}}dx.

Arguing as for
V_{1}
, using (
3.28
), for any
\eta\in[0,1]
, we find

|\partial_{s}V_{2}(t,t+\eta)|\lesssim t^{-\frac{5}{2}}
.

Estimate for
V_{4}
.

The first term in the expression of
V_{4}
in (
3.18
) is rewritten as

V_{4,1}(t,t+\eta)=-\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}t^{2}}\int_{|x-t{\mathbf{e}_{1}}|<t}\frac{x_{1}\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}v_{0}(x+\eta{\mathbf{e}_{1}})}{(t^{2}-|x-t{\mathbf{e}_{1}}|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}}dx.

Arguing as for
V_{1}
, using (
3.28
), for
\eta\in[0,1]
, we find
|\partial_{s}V_{4,1}(t,t+\eta)|\lesssim t^{-\frac{5}{2}}
.
The other terms in the expression of
V_{4}
are estimated similarly.

Estimate for
V_{6}
.

Recall that

\displaystyle V_{6}(t,t+\eta)

\displaystyle=\frac{t^{-\frac{3}{2}}}{4\sqrt{2}\pi^{2}}\left((2t)^{\frac{1}{2}} \Omega[\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}v_{0}(\cdot+\eta\mathbf{e}_{1})](t)-\int_{x_{1}>0}\frac{\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}v_{0}(x+\eta\mathbf{e}_{1})}{x_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}}dx\right).

Thus, by (
3.27
) of Lemma
3.6
, applied to the function
x\mapsto\partial_{x_{1}}^{3}v_{0}(x+\eta{\mathbf{e}_{1}})
, it follows
that for any
\eta\in(0,1)
,

|\partial_{s}V_{6}(t,t+\eta)|\lesssim t^{-\frac{5}{2}}

and the proof of (
3.25
) is complete.
∎

3.3.
Exterior energy in space dimension
6

In this subsection, we consider the linear wave equation in space dimension
6
:

(3.29)

\partial_{t}^{2}u_{L}-\Delta u_{L}=0,\quad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{6},\quad|x|>R+|t|.

With initial data of the form:

(3.30)

\vec{u}_{\restriction t=0}=(0,u_{1}),\quad u_{1}\in L^{2}(\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{6},\;|x|>R\}).

We note that
t/r^{4}
is a radial solution of (
3.29
),(
3.30
), with initial data
(0,1/r^{4})
. We will denote by
\Pi_{R}
the orthogonal projection, on
\mathrm{span}(1/r^{4})
in the space of radial functions in
L^{2}(\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{6},\;|x|>R\})
, and by
\Pi_{R}^{\bot}=\mathrm{Id}-\Pi_{R}
. We will prove:

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
u
be a radial solution of (
3.29
) with initial data of the form (
3.30
). Then

(3.31)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{t+R}^{\infty}|\nabla u_{L}(t,r)|^{2}r^{5}dr=\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{t+R}^{\infty}|\partial_{t}u_{L}(t,r)|^{2}r^{5}dr\\
\geq\frac{3}{20}\int_{R}^{\infty}\left|\Pi_{R}^{\perp}u_{1}(r)\right|^{2}r^{5}dr.

Proof.

This is a simple consequence of Proposition
3.2
.
By using the radiation profile (see Proposition (
A.1
)), it suffices to prove the inequality in (
3.31
). By scaling, we can assume
R=1
. Consider

v(t,r)=\int_{r}^{\infty}\rho\partial_{t}u_{L}(t,\rho)d\rho.

A calculation shows that
v(t,r)
solves the linear wave equation in
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{4}
for
|x|>1+|t|
. Moreover,
\partial_{r}v(0,r)\in L^{2}(\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{4},\;|x|>1\})
and
\partial_{t}v(0,r)=0
since
u_{L}(0,r)=0
. By Proposition
3.2
, we have that

\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{1+t}^{\infty}(\partial_{t}u_{L}(t,r))^{2}r^{5}dr=\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{1+t}^{\infty}(\partial_{r}v(t,r))^{2}r^{3}dr\geq\frac{3}{20}\int_{1+t}^{\infty}\left|\nabla\pi_{1}^{\bot}v(0)\right|^{2}r^{3}dr.

Now
\partial_{r}v(0,r)=-ru_{1}(r)
, and

\partial_{r}(\pi^{\bot}_{1}v(0,r))=\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(v(0,r)-\frac{v(0,1)}{r^{2}}\right)\\
=-ru_{1}(r)+\frac{2}{r^{3}}\int_{1}^{\infty}\rho u_{1}(\rho)d\rho=-r\left(u_{1}(r)-\frac{2}{r^{4}}\int_{1}^{\infty}\rho u_{1}(\rho)d\rho\right),

which by a simple calculation equals
-r\Pi^{\bot}_{1}(u_{1})
, and the proposition follows.
∎

3.4.
Exterior energy for the linear inhomogeneous wave equation

We deduce from Propositions
3.1
and
3.2
similar lower bounds on the exterior energy for the inhomogeneous problem (
3.2
).

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
u_{0}\in\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
and
f\in L^{1}((0,\infty),L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4}))
have radial symmetry.
Let
u
be the solution of (
3.2
). Then

(3.32)

\|u_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{1}}\leq 2\lim_{t\to+\infty}\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^{2}(|x|>t)}+2\left\|1\!\!1_{\{|x|>|t|\}}f\right\|_{L^{1}((0,\infty),L^{2})},

and for
R>0
,

(3.33)

\|\pi_{R}^{\bot}u_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{1}(R)}\leq\sqrt{\frac{20}{3}}\left(\lim_{t\to+\infty}\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^{2}(|x|>t+R)}+\left\|1\!\!1_{\{|x|>|t|+R\}}f\right\|_{L^{1}((0,\infty),L^{2})}\right).

Proof.

Let
v
be the solution of

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}^{2}v-\Delta v&=1\!\!1_{\{|x|>|t|\}}f\\
\vec{v}_{\restriction t=0}&=(0,0).\end{aligned}\right.

On the one hand, by standard energy computations and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, one sees that

\left|\frac{d}{dt}\|\nabla_{t,x}v(t)\|_{L^{2}}\right|\leq\|1\!\!1_{\{|x|>|t|\}}f(t)\|_{L^{2}}

and so by integration on
(0,\infty)
,

\|\nabla_{t,x}v(t)\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|1\!\!1_{\{|x|>|t|\}}f\|_{L^{1}L^{2}},

where in this proof, we denote
\|\cdot\|_{L^{1}L^{2}}=\|\cdot\|_{L^{1}((0,\infty),L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4}))}
.

On the other hand, we observe that the function
u-v
satisfies

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}^{2}(u-v)-\Delta(u-v)&=1\!\!1_{\{|x|\leq|t|\}}f\\
(\vec{u}-\vec{v})_{\restriction t=0}&=(u_{0},0)\end{aligned}\right.

and so
u-v
coincides in the region {
|x|>|t|
} with the solution
u_{L}
of the free wave equation (
3.1
) with initial data
(u_{0},0)
. As a consequence, by Proposition
3.1
,

\displaystyle\|u_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\lim_{t\to+\infty}\|\nabla u_{L}\|_{L^{2}(|x|>t)}

\displaystyle\leq 2\lim_{t\to+\infty}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(|x|>t)}+2\lim_{t\to+\infty}\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}(|x|>t)}

\displaystyle\leq 2\lim_{t\to+\infty}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(|x|>t)}+2\,\|1\!\!1_{\{|x|>|t|\}}f\|_{L^{1}L^{2}},

which completes the proof of (
3.32
).

The proof of (
3.33
) is similar, using the solution
v
of

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}^{2}v-\Delta v&=1\!\!1_{\{|x|>|t|+R\}}f\\
\vec{v}_{\restriction t=0}&=(0,0).\end{aligned}\right.

and Proposition
3.2
instead of Proposition
3.1
.
∎

4.
Rigidity theorem part I: compactly supported pertubations and constant sign solutions

In this section, we will start the proof of Theorems
2
and
1
. We will prove the two theorems at the same time, reducing the co-rotational wave maps equation to a critical wave equation in four space dimension (see §
4.1
). In §
4.2
, we consider solutions with initial data that are equal to a stationary solution for large
r
. In §
4.3
we will treat the case of solutions that have a constant sign outside a wave cone. Section
5
concerns solutions that are odd in time.

4.1.
From co-rotational wave maps to four dimensional waves

Let
R>0
,
(\psi_{0},\psi_{1})\in\mathbf{H}(R)
. Let
\psi
be the solution of (
1.1
) on
\{|x|>R+|t|\}
. Let

u(t,r)=\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\frac{\psi}{r},\;r>R+|t|\quad\text{and}\quad(u_{0},u_{1})(r)=\vec{u}_{\restriction t=0}(r),\;r>R.

Then
(u_{0},u_{1})\in\mathcal{H}(R)
and

\partial_{t}^{2}u-\partial_{r}^{2}u-\frac{3}{r}\partial_{r}u-\frac{1}{r^{2}}u+\frac{\sin(\sqrt{6}ru)}{\sqrt{6}ru}=0,\quad r>R+|t|.

We write this equation as

(4.1)

\partial_{t}^{2}u-\partial_{r}^{2}u-\frac{3}{r}\partial_{r}u=\frac{1}{r^{3}}\Lambda(ru),

where

(4.2)

\Lambda(U)=U-\frac{\sin(\sqrt{6}U)}{\sqrt{6}}.

We note that
\Lambda
is odd, smooth, and that it satisfies:

(4.3)

\forall k\in\{0,1,2\},\quad\left|\left(\frac{d}{dU}\right)^{k}\left(\Lambda(U)-U^{3}\right)\right|\lesssim\min\left(U^{5-k},U^{3-k}\right).

As a consequence, we have the following Lipschitz bound:

(4.4)

\left|\Lambda(U)-\Lambda(V)\right|\lesssim|U-V|(U^{2}+V^{2}).

Note that (
4.1
) with
\Lambda(U)=U^{3}
is exactly the
4D
radial wave equation (and that in this case, (
4.3
) is trivially satisfied).
We have the following small data statement:

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
\Lambda\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, odd, such that (
4.3
) holds. Then there exists
\varepsilon>0
with the following property. For all
(u_{0},u_{1})\in\mathcal{H}(R)
such that
\|u_{L}\|_{(L^{3}L^{6})(R)}\leq\varepsilon
, the solution
u
of (
4.1
) with initial data
(u_{0},u_{1})
is defined for
r>R+|t|
and

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\|\vec{u}(t)-\vec{u}_{L}(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}(R+|t|)}+\|u-u_{L}\|_{(L^{3}L^{6})(R)}\lesssim\|u_{L}\|^{3}_{(L^{3}L^{6})(R)},

where
u_{L}
is the solution of the linear wave equation (
3.1
) with initial data
(u_{0},u_{1})
.

Sketch of proof.

Using equation (
4.1
), the Strichartz estimate outside the cone (
2.6
) and a straightforward bootstrap argument, one first obtains:

\|u\|_{(L^{3}L^{6})(R)}\lesssim\|u_{L}\|_{(L^{3}L^{6})(R)}.

The statement then follows from (
4.1
) and (
2.6
).
∎

Recall that
Q(r)=2\arctan r
is a stationary solution of (
1.1
). By the above reduction, we obtain a solution
\widetilde{W}=\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\frac{1}{r}(\pi-Q)
of (
4.1
) (with
\Lambda
is defined by (
4.2
)), such that for large
r
,

\left|\widetilde{W}(r)-\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{3}r^{2}}\right|\lesssim\frac{1}{r^{4}},\quad r\geq 1.

Rescaling, we obtain a solution
W(r)=\lambda\widetilde{W}(\lambda r)
of (
4.1
) such that

(4.5)

\left|W(r)-\frac{8}{r^{2}}\right|\leq\frac{C}{r^{4}},

that is, with exactly the same asymptotic behaviour as the stationary solution
W
of (
1.7
) defined in the introduction. We denote by
\mathcal{H}(0^{+})
the set of functions
(u_{0},u_{1})
defined on
(0,\infty)
such that

\forall R>0,\quad(u_{0},u_{1})_{\restriction(R,\infty)}\in\mathcal{H}(R),

and define similarly
\dot{H}^{1}(0^{+})
.
Theorems
2
and
1
reduce to the following rigidity theorem:

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
\Lambda\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
odd, such that (
4.3
) holds. Assume that the wave equation (
4.1
) admits a stationary solution
W\in\dot{H}^{1}(0^{+})
such that (
4.5
) holds. Let
(u_{0},u_{1})\in\mathcal{H}(0^{+})
, and assume that
\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,
(u_{0},u_{1})
is not equal to
(\lambda W(\lambda\cdot),0)
.
Then there exists
R>0
such that the solution
u
with initial data
(u_{0},u_{1})
is defined on
\{|x|>R+|t|\}
and

(4.6)

\sum_{\pm}\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\int_{|x|>R+|t|}|\nabla_{t,x}u(t,x)|^{2}dx>0.

Claim 4.3
.

Theorem
4.2
implies Theorems
2
and
1
.

Proof.

The wave equation (
1.7
) is (
4.1
) with
\Lambda(U)=U^{3}
. The ground state
W(x)=(1+|x|^{2}/8)^{-1}
is a stationary solution of (
1.7
) that satisfies (
4.5
). Thus Theorem
4.2
implies that for any solution
u
of (
1.7
) that is not a stationary solution, there exists
R>0
such that
u
is defined for
\{|x|>R+|t|\}
and

(4.7)

\sum_{\pm}\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\int_{|x|>R+|t|}(\partial_{t}u(t,x))^{2}+|\nabla_{x}u(t,x)|^{2}dx>0.

Next, observe that from finite speed of propagation and small data theory, if

\sum_{\pm}\inf_{t\to\pm\infty}\int_{|x|>R+|t|}(\partial_{t}u(t,x))^{2}+|\nabla_{x}u(t,x)|^{2}dx=0,

then (
4.7
) cannot hold, concluding the proof of Theorem
2
.

Let
\psi
as in Theorem
4.2
. Thus
(\psi_{0},\psi_{1})\in\mathbf{H}_{\ell,m}
, which implies
(\psi_{0}-\pi m,\psi_{1})\in\mathbf{H}_{\ell-m,0}
.
Let

u(t,r)=\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\frac{\psi(t,r)-\pi m}{r},\quad(u_{0},u_{1})(r)=\vec{u}(0,r).

Since
(\psi_{0}-\pi m,\psi_{1})\in\mathbf{H}(R)
for all
R
, we have
(u_{0},u_{1})\in\mathcal{H}(0^{+})
. By the assumption on
(\psi_{0},\psi_{1})
, we have that
(u_{0},u_{1})
is not identically
0
, and that it is not equal to
\pm\left(\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{3}r}(\pi-Q(\lambda\cdot)),0\right)
for all
\lambda>0
. By the definition of
W
, we deduce that
(u_{0},u_{1})
satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
4.2
. Furthermore,

\int_{R+|t|}^{+\infty}\left((\partial_{t}u)^{2}+(\partial_{r}u)^{2}\right)r^{3}dr=\frac{2}{3}\int_{R+|t|}^{+\infty}\left((\partial_{t}\psi)^{2}+\left(\partial_{r}\psi-r^{-1}(\psi-\pi m)\right)^{2}\right)rdr\\
\lesssim\int_{R+|t|}\left(\partial_{t}\psi)^{2}+(\partial_{r}\psi)^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}(\psi-\pi m)^{2}\right)rdr,

which shows that the conclusion (
4.6
) of Theorem
4.2
implies

(4.8)

\sum_{\pm}\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\int_{R+|t|}^{\infty}\left((\partial_{t}\psi)^{2}+(\partial_{r}\psi(t,r))^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}(\psi(t,r)-\pi m)^{2}\right)rdr>0.

It remains to prove that (
4.8
) implies the conclusion (
1.13
) of Theorem
1
. This is elementary, but requires some preliminaries on equation (
1.1
) and we postpone the proof to Section
7
, after the proof of Claim
7.1
.
∎

The remainder of this section and the next section are dedicated to the proof of Theorem
4.2
. Until the end of Section
5
,
\Lambda
is an odd, smooth function on
\mathbb{R}
satisfying (
4.3
) and such that there exists a stationary solution
W\in\dot{H}^{1}
of (
4.1
) satisfying (
4.5
).

4.2.
Compactly supported perturbation of a stationary solution

We first prove:

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
(u_{0},u_{1})\in\mathcal{H}(0^{+})
, radial. Assume that there exists
R_{0}>0
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\forall r>R_{0},\quad(u_{0},u_{1})(r)=\left(\lambda W(\lambda r),0\right),

and that the preceding equality does not hold for all
r>0
.
Then there exists
R>0
such that the solution
u
of (
4.1
) with initial data
(u_{0},u_{1})
is defined for
r>R+|t|
and

\sum_{\pm}\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\int_{|x|>R+|t|}|\nabla_{t,x}u(t,x)|^{2}dx>0.

Proof.

Rescaling, we may assume that
\lambda=1
. Let

h=u-W,\quad(h_{0},h_{1})=(u_{0}-W,u_{1})=\vec{h}_{\restriction t=0}.

Then the equation (
4.1
) satisfied by
u
is equivalent to

(4.9)

\partial_{t}^{2}h-\Delta h=\frac{1}{r^{3}}\left(\Lambda(r(W+h))-\Lambda(rW)\right).

Furthermore by (
4.3
)

(4.10)

\frac{1}{r^{3}}\left|\Lambda(r(W+h))-\Lambda(rW)\right|\lesssim|h|(h^{2}+W^{2}).

We define

\rho=\inf\left\{\sigma>0,\;\int_{|x|>\sigma}|\nabla h_{0}|^{2}+h_{1}^{2}=0\right\}

By the assumptions of the proposition,
0<\rho<\infty
.
We fix a small
\varepsilon>0
such that

(4.11)

0<\|(h_{0},h_{1})\|_{\mathcal{H}(\rho-\varepsilon)}\ll 1.

Using the Strichartz estimates (
2.6
), and standard small data theory, we obtain that (
4.9
) has a solution
h
with initial data
(h_{0},h_{1})
defined for
\{|x|>\rho-\varepsilon+|t|\}
, and that

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\|\vec{h}\|_{\mathcal{H}(\rho-\varepsilon+|t|)}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}.

By the radial Sobolev embedding and the property (
4.5
) of
W
, we obtain that there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\forall t\in\mathbb{R},\;\forall r>\rho-\varepsilon+|t|,\quad h^{2}+W^{2}\leq\frac{C}{r^{2}}.

Combined with (
4.10
), and taking a smaller
\varepsilon
if necessary, we see that Proposition 4.7 in
[
23
]
implies that the following holds for all
t>0
or for all
t<0
:

(4.12)

\int_{|x|\geq\rho-\varepsilon+|t|}|\nabla_{t,x}h|^{2}dx\geq\frac{1}{8}\int_{|x|\geq\rho-\varepsilon}\left(|\nabla h_{0}|^{2}+h_{1}^{2}\right)dx>0,

which concludes the proof, since
W\in\dot{H}^{1}(0^{+})
.
∎

Proposition
4.4
reduces the proof of Theorem
4.2
to

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
R_{0}>0
and
u
be a radial solution of (
4.1
) for
|x|>|t|+R_{0}
, with initial data in
\mathcal{H}(R_{0})
. Assume

\sum_{\pm}\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\int_{|x|>R_{0}+|t|}|\nabla_{t,x}u(t,x)|^{2}dx=0.

Then there exists
R\geq R_{0}
such that one of the following holds:

(4.13)

\forall r>R,\quad(u_{0},u_{1})(r)=\left(0,0\right),

or

(4.14)

\exists\lambda>0,\;\iota\in\{\pm 1\},\;\forall r>R,\quad(u_{0},u_{1})(r)=\iota\left(\lambda W(\lambda r),0\right).

The proof of Proposition
4.5
is divided into §
4.3
, and Section
5
.

4.3.
Constant sign solutions

In this section, we prove

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
R_{0}>0
and
u
be a radial solution of (
4.1
) defined for
\{|x|>|t|+R_{0}\}
with

\vec{u}_{\restriction t=0}=(u_{0},u_{1})\in\mathcal{H}(R_{0}).

Assume

(4.15)

\sum_{\pm}\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\int_{|x|>|t|+R_{0}}|\nabla_{t,x}u(t,x)|^{2}dx=0.

Then one of the following holds:

(4.16)

\displaystyle\exists t_{0}\in\mathbb{R},\;\exists R>R_{0},\quad\forall r>|t_{0}|+R,\quad u(t_{0},r)=0

(4.17)

\displaystyle\exists\mu\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\},\;\exists R>R_{0},\;\forall r>R,\quad(u_{0},u_{1})(r)=\left(\mu W(\mu r),0\right).

Proposition
4.6
reduces the proof of Proposition
4.5
to the classification of solutions satisfying (
4.15
) and (
4.16
), which we will carry out in the next section. Note that (
4.16
) is not sufficient to conclude immediately that
u
is identically
0
outside a wave cone, since
\partial_{t}u(t_{0},r)
does not have to vanish for
r>|t_{0}|+R
.

We split the proof of Proposition
4.6
in a few lemmas.

Lemma 4.7
.

The exists a small
\varepsilon_{0}>0
with the following property. Let
u
be a radial solution of (
4.1
) on
\{|x|>R+|t|\}
, where
R\geq 0
. Assume

(4.18)

\displaystyle\int_{|x|>R}|\nabla_{t,x}u(0,x)|^{2}dx\leq\varepsilon_{0}^{2}

(4.19)

\displaystyle\sum_{\pm}\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\int_{|x|>R+|t|}|\nabla_{t,x}u(t,x)|^{2}dx=0.

Then

(4.20)

\displaystyle\forall\rho\geq R,\quad|u_{0}(\rho)|\approx\frac{1}{\rho}\|u\|_{L^{4}(\rho)}\approx\frac{1}{\rho}\|u_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{1}(\rho)}

(4.21)

\displaystyle\forall r\geq\rho\geq R,\quad|u_{0}(r)|\leq 2\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right)^{\frac{11}{6}}|u_{0}(\rho)|.

Proof.

Let

u_{+}(t)=\frac{u(t)+u(-t)}{2},\quad u_{-}(t)=\frac{u(t)-u(-t)}{2}.

Then

(4.22)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \left|\partial_{t}^{2}u_{+}-\Delta u_{+}\right|&\lesssim u_{+}(u_{+}^{2}+u_{-}^{2})\\
\vec{u}_{+\restriction t=0}&=(u_{0},0),\end{aligned}\right.

where we have used that,
\Lambda
being odd,

\left|\Lambda(ru(t))+\Lambda(ru(-t))\right|=\left|\Lambda(ru(t))-\Lambda(-ru(-t))\right|\\
\lesssim r^{3}|u(t)+u(-t)|\left(u^{2}(t)+u^{2}(-t)\right),

by (
4.4
).
By the small data theory for (
4.1
) (see Proposition
4.1
), the condition (
4.18
) implies
\|u\|_{(L^{3}L^{6})(R)}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}^{2}
. Combining with the equation (
4.22
) and Strichartz estimates, we deduce that for all
\rho\geq R
,

(4.23)

\|u_{+}\|_{(L^{3}L^{6})(\rho)}+\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\vec{u}_{+}(t)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}(\rho+|t|)}\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{1}(\rho)}.

Using the lower bound for the exterior energy (Lemma
3.9
) and equation (
4.22
) again, we obtain

(4.24)

\left\|\pi_{\rho}^{\bot}u_{0}\right\|_{\dot{H}^{1}(\rho)}\leq C\left(\|u_{+}^{3}\|_{(L^{1}L^{2})(\rho)}+\|u_{-}^{2}u_{+}\|_{(L^{1}L^{2})(\rho)}\right)\leq C\varepsilon_{0}^{2}\|u_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{1}(\rho)}.

Since

\pi_{\rho}^{\bot}u_{0}(r)=u_{0}(r)-\frac{\rho^{2}}{r^{2}}u_{0}(\rho),\quad\left\|\frac{1}{r^{2}}\right\|_{\dot{H}^{1}(\rho)}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\rho},

we deduce from (
4.24
), taking
\varepsilon_{0}
small enough,

(4.25)

\|u_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{1}(\rho)}\leq C\rho|u_{0}(\rho)|.

Next, we deduce from (
4.24
), (
4.25
) and Sobolev embedding that for
\rho\geq R
,

(4.26)

\left\|u_{0}-\frac{\rho^{2}}{r^{2}}u_{0}(\rho)\right\|_{L^{4}(\rho)}\leq C\varepsilon^{2}_{0}\rho|u_{0}(\rho)|.

This implies, noting that
\left\|\frac{1}{r^{2}}\right\|_{L^{4}(\rho)}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\rho}
,

(4.27)

\left|\|u_{0}\|_{L^{4}(\rho)}-\frac{\rho|u_{0}(\rho)|}{\sqrt{2}}\right|\leq C\varepsilon^{2}_{0}\rho|u_{0}(\rho)|.

Combining with (
4.25
) and Sobolev embedding, we obtain (
4.20
). We also obtain, from (
4.27
),

(4.28)

\|u_{0}\|^{4}_{L^{4}(\rho)}\leq\frac{\rho^{4}}{4^{-}}|u_{0}(\rho)|^{4},

where
4^{-}
is a positive constant, smaller than
4
, that can be chosen arbitrarily close to
4
provided
\varepsilon_{0}
is small enough. Letting

f(\rho)=\int_{\rho}^{+\infty}|u_{0}(r)|^{4}r^{3}dr=\|u_{0}\|^{4}_{L^{4}(\rho)},

we can write (
4.28
) as

(4.29)

f(\rho)\leq-\frac{\rho}{4^{-}}f^{\prime}(\rho),\quad\rho\geq R,

that is

(4.30)

\frac{f^{\prime}(\rho)}{f(\rho)}\leq-\frac{4^{-}}{\rho},\quad\rho\geq R.

Integrating, we deduce that for
r\geq\rho\geq R
,

(4.31)

\frac{f(r)}{f(\rho)}\leq\frac{\rho^{4^{-}}}{r^{4^{-}}},

that is

\|u_{0}\|^{4}_{L^{4}(r)}\leq\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right)^{4^{-}}\|u_{0}\|^{4}_{L^{4}(\rho)}.

Combining with (
4.27
), we deduce (
4.21
).
∎

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
u
,
R
and
\varepsilon_{0}
be as in Lemma
4.7
and assume that for all
t
,
u(t)
is not identically
0
on
[R+|t|,+\infty)
. Then
u
never vanishes and has a constant sign on
\{|x|>R+|t|\}
. Furthermore, for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists
\ell(t)\neq 0
such that

(4.32)

\forall r>R+|t|,\quad\left|r^{2}u(t,r)-\ell(t)\right|\leq Cr^{4}|u(t,r)|^{3}.

Remark 4.9
.

Recall that by the radial Sobolev inequality, for all
t
,
\lim_{r\to\infty}ru(t,r)=0
. Thus (
4.32
), dividing by
r^{2}u(t,r)
, implies

(4.33)

\lim_{r\to\infty}r^{2}u(t,r)=\ell(t).

Proof.

Taking a smaller
\varepsilon_{0}>0
if necessary, we see by finite speed of propagation and the small data theory that for every time
t_{0}
, the function
(t,r)\mapsto u(t+t_{0},r)
satisfies the assumptions of Lemma
4.7
with
R
replaced by
R+|t_{0}|
. By the conclusion of this lemma and the assumption that for all
t
,
u(t,r)
is not identically
0
for
r>R+|t|
, we obtain that
u
does not vanish for
r>R+|t|
. Since
u
is a continuous function, we deduce that it has a constant sign for
r>R+|t|
. We will assume to fix ideas
u(t,r)>0
for all
r>|t|+R
. By (
4.21
),

(4.34)

\forall r>\rho\geq R,\quad 0<u_{0}(r)\leq 2\left(\frac{\rho}{r}\right)^{\frac{11}{6}}u_{0}(\rho).

Next, we observe that for all
(t,r)
with
r>t+R
,

|u_{-}(t,r)|=\frac{1}{2}\left|u(t)-u(-t)\right|\leq\frac{u(t)+u(-t)}{2}=u_{+}(t),

where we have used that
u
is positive. The first inequality in (
4.24
), together with (
4.23
) implies, for
\rho\geq R
,

(4.35)

\left\|\pi_{\rho}^{\bot}u_{0}\right\|_{\dot{H}^{1}(\rho)}\leq C\|u_{+}^{3}\|_{(L^{1}L^{2})(\rho)}\leq C\|u_{0}\|^{3}_{\dot{H}^{1}(\rho)}.

Using (
4.20
) and Sobolev inequalities as in the proof of Lemma
4.7
, we deduce

(4.36)

\left|\|u_{0}\|_{L^{4}(\rho)}-\frac{\rho}{\sqrt{2}}u_{0}(\rho)\right|\leq C\rho^{3}u_{0}(\rho)^{3},\quad\rho\geq R.

Letting as before
f(\rho)=\|u_{0}\|^{4}_{L^{4}(\rho)}
, we deduce, for
r\geq\rho
,

\left|f(r)+\frac{r}{4}f^{\prime}(r)\right|\leq Cr^{6}|u_{0}(r)|^{6}\leq\frac{C}{r^{5}}\rho^{11}u_{0}(\rho)^{6},

that is

\left|\frac{d}{dr}\left(r^{4}f(r)\right)\right|\leq\frac{C}{r^{2}}\rho^{11}u_{0}(\rho)^{6}.

As a consequence,
r^{4}f(r)
has a limit
\ell^{\prime}\geq 0
as
r\to\infty
and

\left|\ell^{\prime}-\rho^{4}f(\rho)\right|\leq C\rho^{10}u_{0}(\rho)^{6},\quad\rho\geq R.

Recall that by (
4.20
),
\rho u_{0}(\rho)\approx f(\rho)^{1/4}
goes to
0
as
\rho\to\infty
. By (
4.36
),

\left|f(\rho)-\frac{\rho^{4}}{4}u_{0}(\rho)^{4}\right|\leq C\rho^{6}u_{0}(\rho)^{6},

and thus

\left|\frac{4\ell^{\prime}}{\rho^{8}}-u_{0}(\rho)^{4}\right|\leq C\rho^{2}|u_{0}(\rho)|^{6}.

This implies
\ell^{\prime}>0
since
u_{0}(\rho)\neq 0
and
\rho u_{0}(\rho)
is small for
\rho
large.
Using the bound
|A-B|\leq\frac{|A^{4}-B^{4}|}{A^{3}}
,
A,B>0
, we also deduce

\left|\frac{\sqrt{2}{\ell^{\prime}}^{\frac{1}{4}}}{\rho^{2}}-u_{0}(\rho)\right|\leq C\rho^{2}|u_{0}(\rho)|^{3},\quad\rho\geq R.

This yields (
4.32
) for
t=0
. Applying this to the solution
(t,r)\mapsto u(t+t_{0},r)
which satisfies the same assumptions as
u
with
R
replaced by
R+|t|
, we deduce (
4.32
) for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
∎

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
u
,
R
,
\varepsilon_{0}
be as in Lemmas
4.7
and

4.8
. Then
\ell
is independent of
t
and

(4.37)

\forall t\in\mathbb{R},\;\forall r\geq R+|t|,\quad|u(t,r)|+\frac{1}{r}\|u(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}(R)}\lesssim\frac{|\ell|}{r^{2}}.

Proof.

We prove that
\ell
is constant by contradiction. Let
(\tau,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and assume
\ell(t)>\ell(\tau)
. Since
\ell
does not vanish, and
u
has a constant sign, we can also assume without loss of generality (using Remark
4.9
),
\ell(\tau)>0
.

Fix a small
\delta>0
, to be specified. By (
4.33
), we have that for large
r

0<(1-\delta)r^{2}u(t,r)\leq\ell(t)\leq(1+\delta)r^{2}u(t,r),

and similarly

0<(1-\delta)r^{2}u(\tau,r)\leq\ell(\tau)\leq(1+\delta)r^{2}u(\tau,r).

As a consequence,

r^{2}(u(t,r)-u(\tau,r))\geq\frac{1}{1+\delta}\ell(t)-\frac{1}{1-\delta}\ell(\tau)=c>0

for
\delta>0
small enough. Thus for large
r
,

(4.38)

u(t,r)-u(\tau,r)\geq\frac{c}{r^{2}}.

Since
u
is a finite energy solution of (
4.1
), on
\{r>R+|t|\}
, we have

u(t)-u(\tau)=\int_{\tau}^{t}\partial_{t}u(s)ds\in L^{2}(\rho)

for large
\rho
, contradicting (
4.38
). Thus
\ell
is independent of
t
.

Going back to (
4.32
), we obtain

\forall\rho>R+|t|,\quad|\rho^{2}u(t,\rho)|-C\rho^{4}|u(t,\rho)|^{3}\leq\ell.

Since by (
4.20
)

\rho|u(t,\rho)|\approx\|\vec{u}(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}(\rho)}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0},\quad\rho\geq R+|t|,

we deduce (
4.37
) using that
\varepsilon_{0}
is small.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.6
.

We let
u
be as in the proposition, and choose
R
large, so that

(4.39)

\int_{|x|>R}|\nabla u_{0}|^{2}+|u_{1}|^{2}dx\leq\varepsilon_{0},

where
\varepsilon_{0}
is small as in the preceding lemmas. We assume that (
4.16
) does not hold so that (taking a larger
R
if necessary),
u
satisfies the assumptions of Lemmas
4.8
and
4.10
. We assume to fix ideas that
u
is positive. Let
\ell
be as in the two Lemmas, so that by (
4.32
), (
4.37
),

(4.40)

\forall t\in\mathbb{R},\;\forall r\geq R+|t|,\quad\left|u(t,r)-\frac{\ell}{r^{2}}\right|\leq C\frac{\ell^{3}}{r^{4}},

for some absolute constant
C>0
.
Rescaling, we may assume that
\ell=8
, so that, by the assumption (
4.5
) on
W
,

(4.41)

\forall t\in\mathbb{R},\;\forall r\geq R+|t|,\quad\left|u(t,r)-W(r)\right|\leq\frac{K}{r^{4}},

for some absolute constant
K>0
. Let, for
\rho\geq R
,

M(\rho)=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}\\
\sigma\geq\rho+|t_{0}|\end{subarray}}\sigma^{2}|u(t_{0},\sigma)-W(\sigma)|.

By (
4.41
),

(4.42)

\forall\rho\geq R,\quad M(\rho)\leq\frac{K}{\rho^{2}}.

We will show that for
R^{\prime}\geq R
large enough,

(4.43)

\forall\rho\geq R^{\prime},\quad M(2\rho)\geq\frac{1}{2}M(\rho).

Note that (
4.42
) and (
4.43
) would imply, for
\rho>R^{\prime}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

M(\rho)\leq 2^{k}M(2^{k}\rho)\leq K\frac{1}{2^{k}\rho^{2}}

and thus
M(\rho)=0
. Thus
u(t,r)=W(r)
for
r\geq R^{\prime}+|t|
, as desired. It remains to prove (
4.43
).

We fix
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\rho\geq R^{\prime}
. We let

u(t)=W+h(t),\quad h_{\pm}(t)=\frac{h(t)\pm h(2t_{0}-t)}{2}.

Using the equation
-\Delta W=\frac{1}{r^{3}}\Lambda(rW)
, and the Taylor expansion

\Lambda(w+\varepsilon)=\Lambda(w)+\Lambda^{\prime}(w)\varepsilon+\Lambda_{2}(w,\varepsilon)\varepsilon^{2},

where

\Lambda_{2}(w,\varepsilon)=\int_{0}^{1}\Lambda^{\prime\prime}(w+\theta\varepsilon)(1-\theta)d\theta,\quad|\Lambda_{2}(w,\varepsilon)|\lesssim|w|+|\varepsilon|,

by (
4.3
). As a consequence, denoting
h_{t_{0}}(t)=h(2t_{0}-t)
,

(4.44)

\partial_{t}^{2}h_{+}-\Delta h_{+}\\
=\frac{1}{2r^{3}}\Big\{\Lambda^{\prime}(rW)(rh_{+})+\Lambda_{2}(rW,rh)(rh)^{2}+\Lambda_{2}(rW,rh_{t_{0}})(rh_{t_{0}})^{2}\Big\},

with the initial data
\vec{h}_{+\restriction t=0}=(u(t_{0})-W,0)
.

We have

(4.45)

|\Lambda^{\prime}(rW)|\lesssim r^{2}W^{2},\quad|\Lambda_{2}(rW,rh)|\lesssim r|W|+r|h|.

Let
\sigma\geq\rho+|t_{0}|
. We have

r\geq\sigma+|t-t_{0}|\Longrightarrow r\geq\max(\rho+|t|,\rho+|2t_{0}-t|),

and thus

(4.46)

r\geq\sigma+|t-t_{0}|\Longrightarrow|h_{+}(t,r)|+|h_{t_{0}}(t,r)|+|h(t,r)|\lesssim\frac{M(\rho)}{r^{2}}.

Combining (
4.44
), (
4.45
), (
4.46
) and the lower bound for the exterior energy (Lemma
3.9
), we obtain

(4.47)

\left\|\pi_{\sigma}^{\bot}h_{+}(t_{0})\right\|_{\dot{H}^{1}(\sigma)}\\
\lesssim\sum_{k=1,2,3}\left\|W\right\|_{(L^{3}L^{6})(\sigma+|t-t_{0}|)}^{3-k}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\sigma+|t-t_{0}|}^{\infty}\frac{M(\rho)^{6}}{r^{9}}dr\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}dt\right)^{\frac{k}{3}}.

Let
\varepsilon(\sigma)=\left\|W\right\|_{(L^{3}L^{6})(\sigma+|t|)}^{2}=\left\|W\right\|_{(L^{3}L^{6})(\sigma+|t-t_{0}|)}^{2}
.
Note that
\varepsilon(\sigma)
is finite, independent of
t_{0}
, and goes to
0
as
\sigma
goes to infinity. The inequality (
4.47
) implies

(4.48)

\left\|h_{+}(t_{0},r)-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{r^{2}}h_{+}(t_{0},\sigma)\right\|_{\dot{H}^{1}_{r}(\sigma)}\leq\frac{C\varepsilon(\sigma)}{\sigma}M(\rho)+\frac{CM(\rho)^{3}}{\sigma^{3}},\quad\sigma\geq\rho+|t_{0}|.

By the radial Sobolev inequality,

\left|h_{+}(t_{0},2\sigma)-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{(2\sigma)^{2}}h_{+}(t_{0},\sigma)\right|\leq\frac{C\varepsilon(\sigma)}{\sigma^{2}}M(\rho)+\frac{CM(\rho)^{3}}{\sigma^{4}}.

As a consequence

\frac{1}{4}|h_{+}(t_{0},\sigma)|\leq\frac{C\varepsilon(\sigma)}{\sigma^{2}}M(\rho)+\frac{CM(\rho)^{3}}{\sigma^{4}}+|h_{+}(t_{0},2\sigma)|\\
\leq\frac{C\varepsilon(\sigma)}{\sigma^{2}}M(\rho)+\frac{CM(\rho)^{3}}{\sigma^{4}}+\frac{M(2\sigma)}{4\sigma^{2}},

where we have used (
4.46
) and the fact that
2\sigma\geq 2\rho+2|t_{0}|\geq 2\rho+|t_{0}|
, so that
|h_{+}(t_{0},2\sigma)|\leq\frac{1}{(2\sigma)^{2}}M(2\sigma)
.

Going back to the definition of
h_{+}
, we deduce

\sigma^{2}|u(t_{0},\sigma)-W(\sigma)|\leq M(2\rho)+C\tilde{\varepsilon}(\rho)M(\rho),

where
\tilde{\varepsilon}(\rho)=\varepsilon(\rho)+\frac{M(\rho)^{2}}{\rho^{2}}
tends to
0
as
\rho
tends to infinity.
This holds for all
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\sigma\geq\rho+|t_{0}|
. Hence

M(\rho)\leq M(2\rho)+C\tilde{\varepsilon}(\rho)M(\rho).

Taking
\rho
large enough, we obtain (
4.43
), concluding the proof.
∎

5.
Rigidity theorem, part II: odd solutions

In this section, we conclude the proof of Proposition
4.5
, and thus of the rigidity theorem (Theorem
4.2
) by the following proposition:

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
R>0
and
u
a radial solution of (
4.1
) on
\{r\geq R+|t|\}
, such that

(5.1)

\displaystyle(u,\partial_{t}u)_{\restriction t=0}=(0,u_{1}),\quad u_{1}\in L^{2}(R)

(5.2)

\displaystyle\sum_{\pm}\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\int_{|x|>R+|t|}|\nabla_{t,x}u(t,x)|^{2}dx=0.

Then

\forall t\in\mathbb{R},\;\forall r>R+|t|,\quad u(t,r)=0.

We first check that Proposition
4.5
(and thus Theorem
4.2
) follows from Propositions
4.6
and
5.1
. Let
u
be as in Proposition
4.5
. Then by Proposition
4.6
, one of the properties (
4.16
) or (
4.17
) holds. We must prove that on of the conclusions (
4.13
) or (
4.14
) of Proposition
4.5
hold. Property (
4.17
) implies immediately (
4.14
). If (
4.16
) holds, we can assume, translating in time and taking a larger
R
, that
u_{0}(0,r)=0
for all
r>R
. Thus
u
satisfies (after a time translation), the assumptions of Proposition
5.1
, and the conclusion of this proposition implies (
4.13
).

We will prove Proposition
5.1
in §
5.3
after giving two preliminary results.
In §
5.1
, we prove a gain of regularity for a solution
u
of (
4.1
) satisfying the assumptions of this proposition. More precisely, we prove that for such a solution,
(\partial_{t}u,\partial_{t}^{2}u)(t)
is in
\mathcal{H}(R^{\prime}+|t|)
for all
t
(where
R^{\prime}
is large) and that
\partial_{t}u
is non-radiative, in the sense that

(5.3)

\sum_{\pm}\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\int_{|x|>R^{\prime}+|t|}|\nabla_{t,x}\partial_{t}u(t,x)|^{2}dx=0.

In §
5.2
, we give an explicit, radial, smooth approximate solution
a
of (
4.1
) on
|x|>|t|
, which satisfies the non-radiative property
(
5.3
) and whose initial data is of the form
(0,a_{1}(r))
, where
a_{1}\notin L^{2}(R)
for
R>0
(indeed
a_{1}(r)
equals
\frac{1}{r^{2}(\log r)^{1/2}}
up to a multiplicative constant).

The proof of Proposition
5.1
in §
5.3
consists in showing that the initial data
u_{1}(r)
of a solution as in Proposition
5.1
is of the same order as or greater than
a_{1}(r)
for large
r
, contradicting the fact that
u_{1}\in L^{2}(R)
. This is done by carefully comparing
u_{1}
with an appropriate rescaling of
a_{1}
, using again the improved lower energy bound of Lemma
3.9
.

5.1.
Gain of regularity

In this subsection we prove:

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
u
be as in Proposition
5.1
. Then there exists a large constant
R^{\prime}\geq R
such that

(5.4)

\displaystyle ru_{1}\in\dot{H}^{1}(R^{\prime})

(5.5)

\displaystyle\forall\rho\geq R^{\prime},\quad\frac{1}{\rho}\|u_{1}\|_{\dot{H}^{1}(\rho)}\approx|u_{1}(\rho)|

Furthermore,
(\partial_{t}u,\partial_{t}^{2}u)
is the restriction to
\{r>R^{\prime}+|t|\}
of a
C^{0}(\mathbb{R},\dot{H}^{1}\times L^{2})
function and

(5.6)

\sum_{\pm}\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\int_{|x|>R^{\prime}+|t|}|\nabla_{t,x}\partial_{t}u|^{2}dx=0.

Proof of Lemma
5.2
.

Step 1: gain of decay.
We let, for
\rho\geq R+|T|
,
T\in\mathbb{R}
,

(5.7)

\eta(T,\rho)=\max_{0\leq t\leq T}\|\partial_{t}u(t)\|_{L^{2}(\rho)}.

In this step, we prove that if
R^{\prime}
is large enough and
\rho\geq R^{\prime}+|T|
, then
r\nabla u(T)\in L^{2}(2\rho)
and

(5.8)

\left\|r\nabla u(T)\right\|_{L^{2}(2\rho)}\lesssim\sqrt{\int_{\rho}^{\infty}|u(T,r)|^{2}r^{3}dr}\lesssim T\eta(T,\rho).

Indeed, using that
u(t,r)=\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{t}u(\tau,r)d\tau
, we have, for all
t\geq 0
and
\rho\geq R+|t|
,

(5.9)

\|u(t)\|_{L^{2}(\rho)}\leq\int_{0}^{t}\|\partial_{t}u(\tau)\|_{L^{2}(\rho)}d\tau\leq|t|\eta(t,\rho).

By Lemma
4.7
, for
\rho>R^{\prime}+|T|
,
R^{\prime}
large,

(5.10)

|u(T,\rho)|\approx\frac{1}{\rho}\|\nabla u(T)\|_{L^{2}(\rho)}.

Integrating, we obtain, for
\sigma>R^{\prime}+|T|
,

\int_{\sigma}^{+\infty}|u(T,\rho)|^{2}\rho^{3}d\rho\approx\int_{\sigma}^{+\infty}\int_{\rho}^{+\infty}|\partial_{r}u(T,r)|^{2}r^{3}dr\rho\,d\rho,

and thus, by Tonelli’s Theorem,

\int_{\sigma}^{\infty}|u(T,\rho)|^{2}\rho^{3}d\rho\approx\int_{\sigma}^{+\infty}(\partial_{r}u(T,r)|^{2}(r^{5}-\sigma^{2}r^{3})dr.

Combining with (
5.9
), we obtain, for
\sigma>R^{\prime}+|t|
,

\int_{2\sigma}^{\infty}|\partial_{r}u(T,r)|^{2}r^{5}dr\lesssim\int_{\sigma}^{\infty}|u(T,r)|^{2}r^{3}dr\lesssim T^{2}\eta(T,\sigma)^{2},

which is exactly (
5.8
).

Step 2. Gain of regularity.

In this step, we prove (
5.4
) and (
5.5
). By (
5.8
), for
T\in[0,1]
,
\rho>R^{\prime}+|T|
,

(5.11)

\int_{2\rho}^{\infty}r^{5}\left|\frac{1}{T}\partial_{r}u(T,r)\right|^{2}dr\lesssim\eta(T,\rho)^{2}\lesssim\eta(1,R^{\prime})^{2}.

As a consequence, there exist
v_{1}\in L^{2}([2R^{\prime},\infty),r^{5}dr)
and a sequence
\{T_{n}\}_{n}
going to
0
as
n
goes to infinity such that

(5.12)

\frac{1}{T_{n}}\partial_{r}u(T_{n})\xrightharpoonup[n\to\infty]{}v_{1}\text{ weakly in }L^{2}\left([2R^{\prime},\infty),r^{5}dr\right).

We have

\frac{1}{T_{n}}u(T_{n},2\rho)=-\frac{1}{T_{n}}\int_{2\rho}^{\infty}\partial_{r}u(T_{n},r)dr,

and thus, using the weak convergence of
\partial_{r}u(T_{n})
,

(5.13)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{T_{n}}u(T_{n},2\rho)=-\int_{2\rho}^{\infty}v_{1}(r)dr,

for all
\rho\geq R^{\prime}
. Next, we observe that by (
5.11
) and a radial Sobolev inequality,

\frac{1}{T_{n}^{2}}|u(T_{n},2\rho)|^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\rho^{4}}\eta(1,R^{\prime})^{2}

for large
n
. This yields by the dominated convergence theorem that the convergence (
5.13
) also holds locally in
L^{2}(R^{\prime},\infty)
. Since

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{t}u(t)\to u_{1}\text{ in }L^{2}(2R),

we deduce, by uniqueness of the limit that for
\rho>R^{\prime}

(5.14)

u_{1}(2\rho)=-\int_{2\rho}^{\infty}v_{1}(r)dr,

where
\int_{R^{\prime}}^{\infty}(v_{1}(r))^{2}r^{5}dr<\infty
. Hence (
5.4
) (taking a larger
R^{\prime}
).

The estimate (
5.5
) follows from (
5.10
), dividing by
T
and letting
T
go to
0
.

Step 3. Proof that
\partial_{t}u
is non-radiative.
In this step we prove (
5.6
).
We fix a small constant
\varepsilon_{0}>0
to be specified. Taking a larger
R^{\prime}
if necessary, we can assume
\|r\partial_{r}u_{1}\|_{L^{2}}+\|u_{1}\|_{H^{1}(R^{\prime})}\leq\varepsilon_{0}
. We define
\tilde{u}_{1}
by

\tilde{u}_{1}(r)=u_{1}(R^{\prime})\text{ if }r<R^{\prime},\quad\tilde{u}_{1}(r)=u_{1}(r)\text{ if }r\geq R^{\prime}.

Using that by the radial Sobolev embedding

|u_{1}(R^{\prime})|\lesssim\frac{1}{(R^{\prime})^{2}}\|r\partial_{r}u_{1}\|_{L^{2}(R^{\prime})}\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{(R^{\prime})^{2}},

we obtain

(5.15)

\|\tilde{u}_{1}\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}.

Let
\tilde{u}
be the solution of (
4.1
) with initial data
(0,\tilde{u}_{1})
at
t=0
. Since
(0,\tilde{u}_{1})\in(\dot{H}^{2}\cap\dot{H}^{1})\times H^{1})
, a standard persistence of regularity argument yields

\overrightarrow{\partial_{t}u}\in C^{0}\left(\mathbb{R},(\dot{H}^{2}\cap\dot{H}^{1})\times H^{1}\right).

We observe that
\partial_{t}\tilde{u}
satisfies the equation

(5.16)

\partial_{t}^{2}(\partial_{t}\tilde{u})-\Delta\partial_{t}\tilde{u}=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Lambda^{\prime}(r\tilde{u})\partial_{t}\tilde{u},

with initial data

(5.17)

\overrightarrow{\partial_{t}\tilde{u}}_{\restriction t=0}=(\tilde{u}_{1},0)\in\dot{H}^{1}\times L^{2}.

Noting that

\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left|\Lambda^{\prime}(r\tilde{u})\right|\lesssim\tilde{u}^{2},\quad\left\|\tilde{u}^{2}\right\|_{(L^{3/2}L^{3})}=\left\|\tilde{u}\right\|_{(L^{3}L^{6})}^{2}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0},

we deduce by Strichartz estimates, and taking
\varepsilon_{0}
small enough,

(5.18)

\|\partial_{t}\tilde{u}\|_{(L^{3}L^{6})}\lesssim\|\tilde{u}_{1}\|_{\dot{H}^{1}}.

As a consequence,
\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Lambda^{\prime}(r\tilde{u})\partial_{t}\tilde{u}\in(L^{1}L^{2})
. By finite speed of propagation,

(5.19)

r>R+|t|\Longrightarrow u(t,r)=\tilde{u}(t,r).

Thus the assumption (
5.2
) implies

\sum_{\pm}\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\int_{|x|>R+|t|}|\nabla_{t,x}\tilde{u}(t,x)|^{2}dx=0.

Combining with Proposition
A.1
in the appendix, we obtain (
5.6
) for
\tilde{u}
and thus, by (
5.19
), for
u
.
∎

5.2.
Approximate non-radiative solution

Lemma 5.3
.

There exists a
C^{\infty}
function
a(t,r)
defined for
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
r>2
such that
\partial_{t}a\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{H}(1))
and

(5.20)

\displaystyle\forall t\in\mathbb{R},\;\forall r>2,\quad a(t,r)=-a(-t,r)

(5.21)

\displaystyle\forall r>2,\quad a_{1}(r):=\partial_{t}a(0,r)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}r^{2}(\log r)^{1/2}}

(5.22)

\displaystyle\forall t\in\mathbb{R},\;\forall r>\max(|t|,2),\quad|a(t,r)|+|t\partial_{t}a(t,r)|\lesssim\frac{|t|}{r^{2}(\log r)^{1/2}}

(5.23)

\displaystyle\sum_{\pm}\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\int_{|x|>|t|}|\nabla_{t,x}\partial_{t}a(t,x)|^{2}dx=0

and
b=\partial_{t}^{2}a-\Delta a-\frac{1}{r^{3}}\Lambda(ra)
satisfies

(5.24)

\forall t\in\mathbb{R},\;\forall r>\max(|t|,2),\quad|b(t,r)|+|t\partial_{t}b(t,r)|\lesssim\frac{|t|}{r^{4}(\log r)^{5/2}}.

Proof.

Let

a(t,r)=\frac{2t}{\sqrt{3}r^{2}(\log r)^{1/2}}-\frac{t^{3}}{3\sqrt{3}r^{4}(\log r)^{3/2}}.

It is easy to see that
a
satisfies (
5.20
), (
5.21
), (
5.22
) and (
5.23
). Using the formula

(5.25)

\Delta\left(\frac{1}{r^{\alpha}(\log r)^{\beta}}\right)=\frac{1}{r^{\alpha+2}}\left(\frac{\alpha(\alpha-2)}{(\log r)^{\beta}}+\frac{(2\alpha-2)\beta}{(\log r)^{\beta+1}}+\frac{\beta(\beta+1)}{(\log r)^{\beta+2}}\right)

we can prove that
b
satisfies (
5.24
). Indeed by (
4.3
),

\displaystyle\left|a^{3}-\frac{1}{r^{3}}\Lambda(ra)\right|\lesssim r^{2}|a|^{5}\lesssim\frac{|t|}{r^{4}(\log r)^{5/2}},\quad r>\max(|t|,2),

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{t}\left(a^{3}-\frac{1}{r^{3}}\Lambda(ra)\right)\right|\lesssim r^{2}a^{4}|\partial_{t}a|\lesssim\frac{1}{r^{4}(\log r)^{5/2}},\quad r>\max(|t|,2),

and

\partial_{t}^{2}a-\Delta a-a^{3}
is a linear combination of:

\displaystyle\frac{t}{r^{4}(\log r)^{5/2}},\quad\frac{t^{3}}{r^{6}(\log r)^{5/2}},\quad\frac{t^{3}}{r^{6}(\log r)^{7/2}},\quad\frac{t^{5}}{r^{8}(\log r)^{5/2}}

\displaystyle\frac{t^{7}}{r^{10}(\log r)^{7/2}},\quad\frac{t^{9}}{r^{12}(\log r)^{9/2}}.

The key point is the double (approximate) cancellation given by:

\partial_{t}^{2}\left(\frac{t^{3}}{3\sqrt{3}r^{4}(\log r)^{3/2}}\right)=-\Delta\left(\frac{2t}{\sqrt{3}r^{2}(\log r)^{1/2}}\right)+l.o.t

and

\Delta\left(\frac{t^{3}}{3\sqrt{3}r^{4}(\log r)^{3/2}}\right)=\left(\frac{2t}{\sqrt{3}r^{2}(\log r)^{1/2}}\right)^{3}+l.o.t.,

where
l.o.t.
is dominated by
t/r^{4}(\log r)^{5/2}
for
r\geq|t|
.
The nonlinearity appears in the last line: the approximate solution
a
is not the perturbation of a linear solution, but a fully nonlinear object.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

An explicit computation shows that

1\!\!1_{\{|x|>1+|t|\}}a\in L^{3}L^{6}.

However
\partial_{t}a(0)
barely fails to be in
L^{2}
. This fact will be crucial in the proof of Proposition
5.1
.

5.3.
Proof of the rigidity result

We are now in position to prove Proposition
5.1
.

Step 1. Preliminaries.

We argue by contradiction, assuming that
u_{1}
is not identically
0
for
r>R
. By (
5.5
) in Lemma
5.2
,
u_{1}
does not change sign, and we can assume that
u_{1}(r)>0
for large
r
. Furthermore, by (
5.4
) in Lemma
5.2
, and the radial Sobolev inequality,

(5.26)

\lim_{r\to\infty}r^{2}u_{1}(r)=0.

We fix
\rho\gg 1
and choose
\lambda=\lambda(\rho)
such that
u_{1}(\rho)=\partial_{t}a_{\lambda}(0,\rho)
, where
a
is the non-radiative approximate solution given by Lemma
5.3
and

a_{\lambda}(t,r)=\lambda a(\lambda t,\lambda r).

In other words,

(5.27)

u_{1}(\rho)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}\rho^{2}\log(\rho\lambda)^{1/2}}.

Note that the equation (
5.27
) always has a unique solution
\lambda(\rho)>1/\rho
, and that by (
5.26
) and (
5.27
),

(5.28)

\lim_{\rho\to\infty}\rho\lambda(\rho)=\infty.

We also note that by (
5.22
),

(5.29)

r>\max(|t|,2/\lambda)\Longrightarrow|a_{\lambda}(t,r)|+|t\partial_{t}a_{\lambda}(t,r)|\lesssim\frac{|t|}{r^{2}\log^{1/2}(r\lambda)}.

We have

(5.30)

\partial_{t}^{2}a_{\lambda}-\Delta a_{\lambda}-\frac{1}{r^{3}}\Lambda(ra_{\lambda})=b_{[\lambda]},

where
b_{[\lambda]}(t,r)=\lambda^{3}b(\lambda t,\lambda r)
satisfies, by (
5.24
),

(5.31)

r>\max(|t|,2/\lambda)\Longrightarrow\left|b_{[\lambda]}(t,r)\right|+\left|t\partial_{t}b_{[\lambda]}(t,r)\right|\lesssim\frac{|t|}{r^{4}\log(\lambda r)^{5/2}}.

Let

w=\partial_{t}a_{\lambda}-\partial_{t}u
,
so that
w
satisfies the equation

(5.32)

\partial_{t}^{2}w-\Delta w=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Lambda^{\prime}(ra_{\lambda})\partial_{t}a_{\lambda}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Lambda^{\prime}(ru)\partial_{t}u+\partial_{t}b_{[\lambda]}

with initial data
w(0)=(\partial_{t}a_{\lambda}(0)-u_{1},0)
.
By explicit computation, using (
5.31
),

(5.33)

\|\partial_{t}b_{[\lambda]}\|_{(L^{1}L^{2})(\rho)}\lesssim\frac{1}{\rho(\log(\lambda\rho))^{5/2}}.

Step 2. Channels of energy.
Fix a small
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, to be specified later. In this step we prove that if
\rho
is chosen large enough,

(5.34)

\left\|u_{1}-\partial_{t}a_{\lambda}(0)\right\|_{\dot{H}^{1}(\rho)}\lesssim\frac{1}{\rho(\log(\lambda\rho))^{5/2}}.

By the equation (
5.32
), Strichartz inequalities and finite speed of propagation,

(5.35)

\|w\|_{(L^{2}L^{8})(\rho)}+\|w\|_{(L^{3}L^{6})(\rho)}+\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\vec{w}(t)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}(\rho+|t|)}\\
\lesssim\|w(0)\|_{\dot{H}^{1}(\rho)}+\left\|\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Lambda^{\prime}(ra_{\lambda})\partial_{t}a_{\lambda}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Lambda^{\prime}(ru)\partial_{t}u+\partial_{t}b_{[\lambda]}\right\|_{(L^{1}L^{2})(\rho)}.

By the exterior energy lower bound of Lemma
3.9
(which also applies to linear solutions outside a wave cone by finite speed of propagation)

(5.36)

\|\pi_{\rho}^{\bot}w(0)\|_{\dot{H}^{1}(\rho)}\lesssim\left\|\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Lambda^{\prime}(ra_{\lambda})\partial_{t}a_{\lambda}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Lambda^{\prime}(ru)\partial_{t}u+\partial_{t}b_{[\lambda]}\right\|_{(L^{1}L^{2})(\rho)}.

Since by the choice of
\lambda
,
w(0,\rho)=0
, we obtain
w(0)=\pi_{\rho}^{\bot}w(0)
. Combining with (
5.33
), (
5.35
) and (
5.36
), we deduce

(5.37)

\|w\|_{(L^{2}L^{8})(\rho)}+\|w\|_{(L^{3}L^{6})(\rho)}+\left\|w_{0}\right\|_{\dot{H}^{1}(\rho)}\\
\lesssim\left\|\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Lambda^{\prime}(ra_{\lambda})\partial_{t}a_{\lambda}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Lambda^{\prime}(ru)\partial_{t}u\right\|_{(L^{1}L^{2})(\rho)}+\frac{1}{\rho(\log(\lambda\rho))^{5/2}},

and we are left with bounding from above the first term in the second line of (
5.37
). We have

(5.38)

\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Lambda^{\prime}(ra_{\lambda})\partial_{t}a_{\lambda}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Lambda^{\prime}(ru)\partial_{t}u=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Lambda^{\prime}(ru)w+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left(\Lambda^{\prime}(ra_{\lambda})-\Lambda^{\prime}(ru)\right)\partial_{t}a_{\lambda}.

By the bound
|\Lambda^{\prime}(U)|\lesssim U^{2}
and Hölder’s inequality,

(5.39)

\left\|\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Lambda^{\prime}(ru)w\right\|_{(L^{1}L^{2})(\rho)}\lesssim\|w\|_{(L^{3}L^{6})(\rho)}\|u\|^{2}_{(L^{3}L^{6})(\rho)}\leq\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{3}\|w\|_{(L^{3}L^{6})(\rho)}

for large
\rho
. Next, we have, by the bound
|\Lambda^{\prime\prime}(U)|\lesssim|U|
which implies
|\Lambda^{\prime}(U)-\Lambda^{\prime}(V)|\lesssim|U-V|\left(|U|+|V|\right)
,

(5.40)

\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left|\Lambda^{\prime}(ra_{\lambda})-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Lambda^{\prime}(ru)\right|\lesssim|u||u-a_{\lambda}|+|a_{\lambda}||u-a_{\lambda}|.

We will bound separately
\left\|u(u-a_{\lambda})\partial_{t}a_{\lambda}\right\|_{(L^{1}L^{2})(\rho)}
and

\left\|a_{\lambda}(u-a_{\lambda})\partial_{t}a_{\lambda}\right\|_{(L^{1}L^{2})(\rho)}
;
By Hölder inequality,

\left\|u\partial_{t}a_{\lambda}(u-a_{\lambda})\right\|_{(L^{1}L^{2})(\rho)}\lesssim\|u\|_{(L^{2}L^{8})(\rho)}\|\partial_{t}a_{\lambda}(u-a_{\lambda)}\|_{(L^{2}L^{8/3})(\rho)}.

Furthermore, using the bound (
5.29
), and the fact that
w=\partial_{t}(a_{\lambda}-u)
, we obtain for large
\rho

\left|\partial_{t}a_{\lambda}(u-a_{\lambda})\right|\lesssim\frac{1}{r^{2}\log(r\lambda)^{1/2}}\left|\int_{0}^{t}w(\tau,r)d\tau\right|\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{r^{2}}\left|\int_{0}^{t}w(\tau,r)d\tau\right|,

where we have used (
5.28
). By Lemma
C.1
in Appendix
C
,

\left\|\partial_{t}a_{\lambda}(u-a_{\lambda})\right\|_{(L^{2}L^{8/3})(\rho)}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}\|w\|_{(L^{2}L^{8})(R)},

and thus

(5.41)

\left\|u\partial_{t}a_{\lambda}(u-a_{\lambda})\right\|_{(L^{1}L^{2})(\rho)}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}\|w\|_{(L^{2}L^{8})(\rho)}.

Using again the bound (
5.29
) on
a_{\lambda}
and
\partial_{t}a_{\lambda}
, we obtain

\left|a_{\lambda}\partial_{t}a_{\lambda}(u-a_{\lambda})\right|\lesssim\frac{|t|}{r^{4}\log(\lambda r)}\left|\int_{0}^{t}w(\tau,r)d\tau\right|.

By Lemma
C.2
in Appendix
C
,

(5.42)

\left\|a_{\lambda}\partial_{t}a_{\lambda}(u-a_{\lambda})\right\|_{(L^{1}L^{2})(\rho)}\lesssim\frac{1}{(\log(\lambda\rho))^{1/3}}\|w\|_{(L^{3}L^{6})(\rho)}.

Combining (
5.37
), (
5.38
), (
5.39
), (
5.41
) and (
5.42
), we deduce that for large
\rho
,

(5.43)

\|w\|_{(L^{2}L^{8})(\rho)}+\|w\|_{(L^{3}L^{6})(\rho)}+\left\|w_{0}\right\|_{\dot{H}^{1}(\rho)}\lesssim\\
\varepsilon_{0}\left(\|w\|_{(L^{2}L^{8})(\rho)}+\|w\|_{(L^{3}L^{6})(\rho)}\right)+\frac{1}{\rho(\log(\lambda\rho))^{5/2}}.

This implies (
5.34
) if
\varepsilon_{0}
is small enough.

Step 3. Differential inequality.

In this step, we deduce from (
5.34
) the following inequality for large
\rho
:

(5.44)

\left|\|u_{1}\|_{L^{4}(\rho)}^{4}-\frac{\rho^{4}u_{1}(\rho)^{4}}{4}\right|\lesssim\rho^{8}u_{1}(\rho)^{6}.

Recall that
\rho^{2}u_{1}(\rho)
is small when
\rho
is large (see (
5.26
)), so that
\rho^{8}u_{1}(\rho)^{6}\ll\rho^{4}u_{1}(\rho)^{4}
.
The inequality (
5.44
) can be seen as an approximate differential equation on the
L^{4}
norm of
u
. We will deduce a contradiction from this inequality in the next step.

By (
5.34
) and the critical Sobolev inequality, we obtain, for
\rho
large

(5.45)

\left\|u_{1}-\partial_{t}a_{\lambda}(0)\right\|_{L^{4}(\rho)}\lesssim\frac{1}{\rho(\log(\lambda\rho))^{5/2}}.

Using the explicit value of
\partial_{t}a_{\lambda}(0)
, we have

\left\|\partial_{t}a_{\lambda}(0)\right\|_{L^{4}(\rho)}^{4}=\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\right)^{4}\int_{\rho}^{\infty}\frac{dr}{r^{5}\log^{2}(r\lambda)},

which yields, after an integration by parts,

(5.46)

\left\|\partial_{t}a_{\lambda}\right\|_{L^{4}(\rho)}^{4}=\frac{4}{9\rho^{4}\log^{2}(\rho\lambda)}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\rho^{4}\log^{3}(\rho\lambda)}\right).

By (
5.45
),
\left|\|u_{1}\|_{L^{4}(\rho)}-\|\partial_{t}a_{\lambda}(0)\|_{L^{4}(\rho)}\right|\lesssim\frac{1}{\rho\log(\lambda\rho)^{5/2}}
. Thus

\|u_{1}\|_{L^{4}(\rho)}\sim\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\frac{1}{\rho\left(\log(\rho\lambda)\right)^{1/2}}

and we deduce from (
5.45
)

(5.47)

\left|\|u_{1}\|_{L^{4}(\rho)}^{4}-\frac{4}{9\rho^{4}\log^{2}(\rho\lambda)}\right|\lesssim\frac{1}{\rho^{4}\log^{3}(\rho\lambda)}.

Going back to the definition of
\lambda
(see (
5.27
)), we deduce (
5.44
)).

Step 4. Lower bound on
u_{1}
and contradiction.

In this step, we deduce from (
5.44
) and the fact that
\rho^{2}u_{1}(\rho)
goes to
0
that for some constant
C>0
,

(5.48)

u_{1}(\rho)\geq\frac{1}{C\rho^{2}(\log\rho)^{1/2}},\quad\rho\to\infty,

a contradiction since
\rho\mapsto\frac{1}{\rho^{2}(\log\rho)^{1/2}}
is not in
L^{2}(r^{3}dr)
.

Let

f(\rho)=\|u_{1}\|^{4}_{L^{4}(\rho)}.

Since by (
5.44
),
f(\rho)\sim\frac{\rho^{4}u_{1}(\rho)^{4}}{4}
, we have

(5.49)

\lim_{\rho\to\infty}\rho^{4}f(\rho)=0.

Also, we can rewrite (
5.44
) as

(5.50)

\left|f(\rho)+\frac{\rho}{4}f^{\prime}(\rho)\right|\lesssim f(\rho)^{3/2}\rho^{2}.

Next, we let
g(\rho)=\rho^{4}f(\rho)
and note that by (
5.49
),

(5.51)

\lim_{\rho\to\infty}g(\rho)=0.

Since
f^{\prime}(\rho)=-\frac{4}{\rho^{5}}g(\rho)+\frac{1}{\rho^{4}}g^{\prime}(\rho)
, the estimate (
5.50
) reads

(5.52)

\left|\frac{g^{\prime}(\rho)}{g(\rho)^{3/2}}\right|\lesssim\frac{1}{\rho}.

Integrating between
\rho_{0}
and
\rho>\rho_{0}
, where
\rho_{0}
is large, we deduce that for large
\rho
,

\frac{1}{g(\rho)^{1/2}}\leq C\log\rho,

where
C
is a large positive constant (depending on
g
). This implies that for large
\rho

g(\rho)\geq\frac{1}{C(\log\rho)^{2}}.

Since
g(\rho)=\rho^{4}f(\rho)
and by (
5.44
),
f(\rho)\approx\rho^{4}u_{1}(\rho)^{4}
, we deduce (
5.48
) as announced.
∎

6.
Soliton resolution for the wave equation

In this Section we prove Theorem
4
. We start with some preliminaries on profile decomposition for the wave equations (
3.1
) and (
1.7
).

6.1.
Profile decomposition

Let
\big\{(u_{0,n},u_{1,n})\big\}_{n}
be a bounded sequence of radial functions in
\mathcal{H}
. We say that it admits a profile decomposition if for all
j\geq 1
, there exist a solution
U^{j}_{L}
to the free wave equation with initial data in
\mathcal{H}
and sequences of parameters
\{\lambda_{j,n}\}_{n}\in(0,\infty)^{\mathbb{N}}
,
\{t_{j,n}\}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
such that

(6.1)

j\neq k\Longrightarrow\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\lambda_{j,n}}{\lambda_{k,n}}+\frac{\lambda_{k,n}}{\lambda_{j,n}}+\frac{|t_{j,n}-t_{k,n}|}{\lambda_{j,n}}=+\infty,

and, denoting

(6.2)

\displaystyle U^{j}_{L,n}(t,r)=\frac{1}{\lambda_{j,n}}U^{j}_{L}\left(\frac{t-t_{j,n}}{\lambda_{j,n}},\frac{r}{\lambda_{j,n}}\right),\quad j\geq 1

(6.3)

\displaystyle w_{n}^{J}(t)=S_{L}(t)(u_{0,n},u_{1,n})-\sum_{j=1}^{J}U^{j}_{L,n}(t),

one has

(6.4)

\lim_{J\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|w_{n}^{J}\|_{L^{3}L^{6}}=0.

We recall (see
[
2
]
,
[
4
]
) that any bounded sequence in
\mathcal{H}
has a subsequence that admits a profile decomposition. The properties above imply that the following weak convergences hold:

(6.5)

j\leq J\Longrightarrow\left(\lambda_{j,n}w_{n}^{J}\left(t_{j,n},\lambda_{j,n}\cdot\right),\lambda_{j,n}^{2}\partial_{t}w_{n}^{J}\left(t_{j,n},\lambda_{j,n}\cdot\right)\right)\xrightharpoonup[n\to\infty]{}0\text{ in }\mathcal{H}.

Furthermore we have the Pythagorean expansions valid, for all
J\geq 1

(6.6)

\displaystyle\|(u_{0,n},u_{1,n})\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\left\|\vec{U}^{j}_{L}(0)\right\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}+\left\|\vec{w}_{n}^{J}(0)\right\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}+o_{n}(1)

(6.7)

\displaystyle E(u_{0,n},u_{1,n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{J}E(\vec{U}_{L,n}^{j}(0))+E(\vec{w}_{n}^{J}(0))+o_{n}(1).

If
\{(u_{0,n},u_{1,n})\}_{n}
admits a profile decomposition, we can assume, extracting subsequences and time-translating the profiles if necessary, that the following limits exist:

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{-t_{j,n}}{\lambda_{j,n}}=\tau_{j}\in\{-\infty,0,\infty\}.

If
\tau_{j}=0
, we will assume without loss of generality
t_{j,n}=0
for all
n
.
If
\tau_{j}=0
, we define
U^{j}
as the unique solution to the nonlinear wave equation (
1.7
) such that

(6.8)

\vec{U}^{j}(0)=\vec{U}^{j}_{L}(0).

If
\tau_{j}\in\{\pm\infty\}
, we simply let
U^{j}=U_{L}^{j}
. We denote by
U^{j}_{n}
the rescaled profile:

U^{j}_{n}(t,r)=\frac{1}{\lambda_{j,n}}U^{j}\left(\frac{t-t_{j,n}}{\lambda_{j,n}},\frac{r}{\lambda_{j,n}}\right).

We will now state a superposition principle (sometime called
nonlinear profile decomposition
) outside wave cones using the profiles
U^{j}
. We denote by
\mathcal{J}
the set of indices
j\geq 1
such that
\tau_{j}=0
and the solution
U^{j}
of (
1.7
) with initial data
(U_{0}^{j},U_{1}^{j})
cannot be extended to a solution with finite
L^{3}L^{6}
norm to the wave cone
\{|x|>|t|\}
. By the small data theory and (
6.6
),
\mathcal{J}
is finite. If
j\notin\mathcal{J}
, then the solution
U_{n}^{j}(t,x)
is well defined for
\{|x|>|t|\}
and

(6.9)

\sup_{n\to\infty}\|U_{n}^{j}\|_{(L^{3}L^{6})(\{|x|>|t|\})}<\infty.

If
\mathcal{J}
is empty, we let
R_{n}=0
for all
n
. If not, after extraction, (
6.1
) implies that there is a unique
j_{0}\in\mathcal{J}
such that

\forall j\in\mathcal{J}\setminus\{j_{0}\},\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\lambda_{j,n}}{\lambda_{j_{0},n}}=0.

In this case, we fix a
R>0
such that
U^{j_{0}}
is defined (with a finite
L^{3}L^{6}
norm) in
\{|x|>R+|t|\}
, and we let
R_{n}=R\lambda_{j_{0},n}
for all
n
.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
\{(u_{0,n},u_{1,n})\}_{n}
,
U^{j}_{n}
,
w_{n}^{J}
,
R_{n}
be a above. Then for large
n
, there is a solution
u_{n}
of (
1.7
) defined in
\{r>R_{n}+|t|\}
with initial data
\{(u_{0,n},u_{1,n})\}_{n}
at
t=0
. Furthermore, denoting, for
J\geq 1
,
r>R_{n}+|t|
,

\epsilon_{n}^{J}(t,r)=u_{n}(t,r)-\sum_{j=1}^{J}U_{n}^{j}(t,r)-w_{n}^{J}(t,r),

we have

\lim_{J\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\left[\|\epsilon_{n}^{J}\|_{(L^{3}L^{6})(R_{n})}+\sup_{t\geq 0}\left\|\vec{\epsilon}_{n}^{J}(t)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}(t+R_{n})}\right]=0.

Proof.

The proof uses

\displaystyle\forall j\geq 1,\quad\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|U_{n}^{j}\|_{(L^{3}L^{6})(R_{n})}<\infty

\displaystyle\forall j,k,\ell\geq 1,\quad j\neq k\Longrightarrow\left\|U_{n}^{j}U_{n}^{k}U_{n}^{\ell}\right\|_{(L^{1}L^{2})(R_{n})}=0,

and long-time perturbation theory arguments.

We omit the details, since they are similar to the proof when the solution is not restricted to the exterior of a wave cone (see e.g. the Main Theorem p. 135 in
[
2
]
in the defocusing case, and a sketch of proof in the focusing case right after Proposition 2.8 in
[
17
]
). The proof is also similar to the one of the corresponding result for wave maps which is detailed below.
∎

We next state a pseudo-orthogonality property of the profiles that will be needed in the proof of Theorem
4
. See Appendix
B
for the proof.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\{(u_{0,n},u_{1,n})\}_{n}
,
U^{j}_{n}
,
w_{n}^{J}
,
R_{n}
be a above. Let
\{s_{n}\}_{n}
be a sequence of times,
\rho_{n},\rho^{\prime}_{n}>0
be such that
R_{n}+|s_{n}|\leq\rho_{n}<\rho^{\prime}_{n}
(
\rho_{n}^{\prime}=\infty
is allowed). Then

(6.10)

\displaystyle j\neq k

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\rho_{n}<|x|<\rho_{n}^{\prime}}\nabla_{t,x}U_{n}^{j}(s_{n},x)\cdot\nabla_{t,x}U_{n}^{k}(s_{n},x)dx=0

(6.11)

\displaystyle j\leq J

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\rho_{n}<|x|<\rho_{n}^{\prime}}\nabla_{t,x}U_{n}^{j}(s_{n},x)\cdot\nabla_{t,x}w_{n}^{J}(s_{n},x)dx=0.

6.2.
Finite time blow-up case

In this subsection we deduce the finite time blow-up case of Theorem
4
from the rigidity theorem, Theorem
2
. The proof is inspired by the one of the analogous result in space dimension 3 (see
[
20
]
). However, unlike in space dimension 3, we will need the fact (proved in
[
10
]
) that the soliton resolution holds for a sequence of times.

We consider a solution
u
of (
1.7
), with initial data (
1.8
) such that
T_{+}=T_{+}(u)
is finite and

(6.12)

\liminf_{t\overset{<}{\longrightarrow}T_{+}}\left\|\vec{u}(t)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}<\infty

6.2.1.
Convergence of the solution

We first recall that the solution
u
converges outside the wave cone:

Lemma 6.3
.

There exists
(v_{0},v_{1})\in\mathcal{H}
such that

\lim_{t\to T_{+}}\int_{|x|>|T_{+}-t|}|\nabla(u(t,x)-v_{0}(x))|^{2}+(\partial_{t}u(t,x)-v_{1}(x))^{2}dx=0.

Lemma
6.3
is identical to Lemma 4.1 in
[
20
]
. We refer to this article for the proof. Note that
[
20
, Lemma 4.1]
is stated in space dimension
3
, however the proof is independent of the dimension.

6.2.2.
Analysis along a sequence of times

The core of the proof of Theorem
4
in the finite time blow-up case is the following proposition, that we will deduce from Theorem
2
:

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
\{t_{n}\}_{n}
be a sequence of times such that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}t_{n}=T_{+},\quad\forall n,\;0\leq t_{n}<T_{+}

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\vec{u}(t_{n})\|_{\mathcal{H}}<\infty.

Then there exists a subsequence of
\{t_{n}\}_{n}
(that we still denote by
\{t_{n}\}_{n}
),
J\geq 0
,
(\iota_{j})_{j}\in\{\pm 1\}^{J}
, sequences
\{\lambda_{j,n}\}_{n}
,
1\leq j\leq J
with

\lambda_{1,n}\ll\ldots\ll\lambda_{J,n}\ll T_{+}-t_{n},

such that

(6.13)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|u(t_{n})-v_{0}-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\frac{\iota_{j}}{\lambda_{j,n}}W\left(\frac{\cdot}{\lambda_{j,n}}\right)\right\|_{\dot{H}^{1}}=0

(6.14)

\displaystyle E(u_{0},u_{1})=E(v_{0},v_{1})+JE(W,0)+\frac{1}{2}\left\|\partial_{t}u(t_{n})-v_{1}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+o_{n}(1),

where
(v_{0},v_{1})
are given by Lemma
6.3
.

Proposition
6.4
is the analog in space dimension 4 of Proposition 4.2 of
[
20
]
. However the conclusion (
6.13
) of Proposition
6.4
is weaker than the one of Proposition 4.2 of
[
20
]
, which states in addition that
\partial_{t}u(t_{n})-v_{1}
goes to
0
as
n
goes to infinity in
L^{2}
. This weaker conclusion is due to the fact that in space dimension
4
, the lower bound for the asymptotic linear energy (
3.4
) is weaker than its analog in space dimension
3
.

Proof.

To simplify notations, we assume without loss of generality in all the proof that

T_{+}(u)=1.

Step 1.
We first prove that we can assume

(6.15)

\|(v_{0},v_{1})\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\varepsilon,

where
\varepsilon
is a fixed small constant. Let
v
be the solution of (
1.7
) such that
\vec{v}(1)=(v_{0},v_{1})
. By finite speed of propagation and the definition of
(v_{0},v_{1})
,
v(t,r)=u(t,r)
for
r>1-t
,
t<1
close to
1
. Thus

\int_{1-t}^{3-3t}\left((\partial_{t,r}u(t,r))^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}(u(t,r))^{2}\right)r^{3}dr\\
=\int_{1-t}^{3-3t}\left((\partial_{t,r}v(t,r))^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}(v(t,r))^{2}\right)r^{3}dr\underset{t\to 1}{\longrightarrow}0.

Hence, we can choose
t_{0}<1
close to
1
such that

(6.16)

t_{0}<t<1\Longrightarrow\int_{1-t}^{3-3t}\left((\partial_{t,r}u(t,r))^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}(u(t,r))^{2}\right)r^{3}dr\leq\varepsilon^{2}.

We fix
(\tilde{u}_{0},\tilde{u}_{1})\in\mathcal{H}
such that

(6.17)

\displaystyle r\leq 3-3t_{0}\Longrightarrow(\tilde{u}_{0},\tilde{u}_{1})(r)=(u(t_{0},r),\partial_{t}u(t_{0},r))

(6.18)

\displaystyle\int_{1-t_{0}}^{\infty}\left((\partial_{r}\tilde{u}_{0}(r))^{2}+(\tilde{u}_{1}(r))^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}(\tilde{u}_{0}(r))^{2}\right)r^{3}dr\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}.

Let
\tilde{u}
be the solution of (
1.7
) such that
(\tilde{u}(t_{0}),\partial_{t}\tilde{u}(t_{0}))=(\tilde{u}_{0},\tilde{u}_{1})
. By (
6.17
) and finite speed of propagation,

(6.19)

\tilde{u}(t,r)=u(t,r),\quad r\leq 3-2t_{0}-t,\quad t_{0}<t<1.

By finite speed of propagation, small data theory and (
6.18
),

(6.20)

\left\|(\tilde{u},\partial_{t}\tilde{u})\right\|_{\mathcal{H}(1-2t_{0}+t)}\lesssim\varepsilon,\quad t_{0}<t<1.

Combining (
6.16
), (
6.19
) and (
6.20
), we see that

\|(\tilde{u},\partial_{t}\tilde{u})(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}(1-t)}\lesssim\varepsilon,\quad t_{0}<t<1.

Replacing
u
by
\tilde{u}
, we obtain a solution of (
1.7
) that blows up at
t=1
, has not changed in
\{r<1-t\}
and satisfies the additional condition (
6.15
). Note that the fact that
u=\tilde{u}
in
\{r<1-t\}
implies (defining
\tilde{v}
in the same way as
v
with
u
replaced by
\tilde{u}
)

\lim_{t\to 1}\big\|(u(t)-v(t))-(\tilde{u}(t)-\tilde{v}(t)),\partial_{t}u(t)-\partial_{t}v(t))-(\partial_{t}\tilde{u}(t)-\partial_{t}\tilde{v}(t))\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}=0,

so that if (
6.13
), (
6.14
) hold for
\tilde{u}
, then it holds for the original
u
.

Step 2. Profile decomposition.
Extracting subsequences, we assume that
\vec{u}(t_{n})-(v_{0},v_{1})
admits a profile decomposition as in §
6.1
. We use the same notations
U^{j}_{L}
,
U^{j}
,
\{\lambda_{j,n}\}_{n}
,
\{t_{j,n}\}_{n}
,
w_{n}^{J}
as in §
6.1
. Extracting subsequences again and rescaling the profiles, we can assume that there is a partition
\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}=\mathcal{I}_{s}\cup\mathcal{I}_{c}\cup\mathcal{I}_{+}\cup\mathcal{I}_{-}
of the set of indices such that

\displaystyle j\in\mathcal{I}_{s}

\displaystyle\iff\forall n,\;t_{j,n}=0,U_{L}^{j}(0)\in\{W,-W,0\}\text{ and }\partial_{t}U_{L}^{j}(0)=0

\displaystyle j\in\mathcal{I}_{c}

\displaystyle\iff\forall n,\;t_{j,n}=0\text{ and }U^{j}\text{ is not a stationary solution of \eqref{NLW}}

\displaystyle j\in\mathcal{I}_{\pm}

\displaystyle\iff\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{-t_{j,n}}{\lambda_{j,n}}=\pm\infty.

In Step 3, we prove that
\mathcal{I}_{c}=\emptyset
. In Step 4, we prove that
\mathcal{I}_{\pm}=\emptyset
. In Step 5, we conclude the proof, by showing that the first component of the dispersive remainder
w_{n}^{J}
goes to
0
in
\dot{H}^{1}
as
n\to\infty
.

Step 3. Compact profiles.
In this step we prove by contradiction that
\mathcal{I}_{c}
is empty. Assume that
\mathcal{I}_{c}
is not empty. As in Subsection
6.1
, we denote by
\mathcal{J}
the set of indices
j\geq 1
such that
\forall n
,
t_{j,n}=0
and the solution
U^{j}
cannot be extended to
\{r>|t|\}
with finite
L^{3}L^{6}
norm. We recall that by the small data theory,
\mathcal{J}
is finite, and note that
\mathcal{J}\subset\mathcal{I}_{c}
. If
\mathcal{J}
is not empty, we can extract subsequences such that there exists a unique
j_{0}\in\mathcal{J}
such that

\forall j\in\mathcal{J}\setminus\{j_{0}\},\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\lambda_{j,n}}{\lambda_{j_{0},n}}=0.

By Theorem
2
, there exists
R>0
such that the solution
U^{j_{0}}
is defined for
|x|>R+|t|
and

(6.21)

\sum_{\pm\infty}\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\int_{|x|>R+|t|}\left|\nabla_{t,x}U^{j_{0}}(t,x)\right|^{2}\,dx=\eta_{0}>0.

If
\mathcal{J}
is empty, we let
j_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{c}
and
R=0
, so that
U^{j_{0}}
is defined on
\{|x|>|t|\}=\{|x|>R+|t|\}
and, by Theorem
2
, (
6.21
) holds.

In both cases, combining (
6.21
) with finite speed of propagation and the small data theory, we see that
the following holds for all
t>0
or for all
t<0
:

(6.22)

\int_{|x|>R+|t|}|\nabla_{t,x}U^{j_{0}}(t,x)|^{2}dx\geq\eta_{1}>0.

We let
R_{n}=R\lambda_{j_{0},n}
for all
n
. By Proposition
6.1
, for large
n
, the solution
u
is defined for
\{r>R_{n}+|t-t_{n}|\}
and

(6.23)

u(t_{n}+\tau,r)=v(1+\tau,r)+\sum_{j=1}^{J}U_{n}^{j}(\tau,r)+w_{n}^{J}(\tau,r)+\varepsilon_{n}^{J}(\tau,r),

where

(6.24)

\lim_{J\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\tau}\left\|\varepsilon_{n}^{J}\right\|_{\mathcal{H}(R_{n}+|\tau|)}=0,

and
v
is as above the solution of (
1.7
) with initial data
(v_{0},v_{1})
at
t=1
, which is globally defined and scattering by the small data theory. Using the continuity of
v
, we can rewrite (
6.23
) as

(6.25)

u(t_{n}+\tau,r)=v(t_{n}+\tau,r)+\sum_{j=1}^{J}U_{n}^{j}(\tau,r)+w_{n}^{J}(\tau,r)+\varepsilon_{n}^{J}(\tau,r),

where the remainder
\varepsilon_{n}^{J}
has slightly changed but still satisfies (
6.24
).
We distinguish two cases.

Case 1. Channels of energy in the future.
We assume that (
6.22
) holds for all
t>0
. Recall that by finite speed of propagation
u(t,r)=v(t,r)
for
r>1-t
,
t
close to
1
.
By (
6.25
) at
\tau=1-t_{n}
, we obtain, denoting
\rho_{n}=R\lambda_{j_{0},n}+1-t_{n}
,

(6.26)

0=\sum_{1\leq j\leq J}\int_{|x|>\rho_{n}}\nabla_{t,x}U_{n}^{j}(1-t_{n},x)\cdot\nabla_{t,x}U_{n}^{j_{0}}(1-t_{n},x)\,dx\\
+\int_{|x|>\rho_{n}}\nabla_{t,x}w_{n}^{J}(1-t_{n},x)\cdot\nabla_{t,x}U_{n}^{j_{0}}(1-t_{n},x)dx\\
+\int_{|x|>\rho_{n}}\nabla\varepsilon_{n}^{J}(1-t_{n},x)\cdot\nabla_{t,x}U_{n}^{j_{0}}(1-t_{n},x)dx.

By (
6.22
), at
t=(1-t_{n})/\lambda_{j_{0},n}
,

(6.27)

\int_{|x|>\rho_{n}}|\nabla_{t,x}U_{n}^{j_{0}}(1-t_{n},x)|^{2}dx\\
=\int_{|y|>R+\frac{1-t_{n}}{\lambda_{j_{0},n}}}\left|\nabla_{t,x}U^{j_{0}}\left(\frac{1-t_{n}}{\lambda_{j_{0},n}},y\right)\right|^{2}dy\geq\frac{\eta_{1}}{2}

for large
n
. On the other hand, by Lemma
6.2
,

(6.28)

\displaystyle j\neq j_{0}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{|x|>\rho_{n}}\nabla_{t,x}U_{n}^{j}(1-t_{n},x)\cdot\nabla_{t,x}U_{n}^{j_{0}}(1-t_{n},x)\,dx=0

(6.29)

\displaystyle J\geq j_{0}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{|x|>\rho_{n}}\nabla_{t,x}w_{n}^{J}(1-t_{n},x)\cdot\nabla_{t,x}U_{n}^{j_{0}}(1-t_{n},x)\,dx=0.

Combining (
6.26
), (
6.27
), (
6.28
), (
6.29
) with the property (
6.24
) of
\varepsilon_{n}^{J}
, we obtain a contradiction.

Case 2. Channels of energy in the past.
Next, we assume that (
6.22
) holds for all
t\leq 0
. We fix
t_{0}<1
in the domain of existence of
u
. By (
6.25
), at
\tau=t_{0}-t_{n}
, we have, letting
\tau_{n}=t_{0}-t_{n}
,
\rho_{n}=R\lambda_{j_{0},n}+|\tau_{n}|
.

(6.30)

\sum_{j=1}^{J}\int_{|x|>\rho_{n}}\nabla U_{n}^{j}(\tau_{n},x)\cdot\nabla U_{n}^{j_{0}}(\tau_{n},x)dx\\
+\int_{|x|>\rho_{n}}\nabla_{t,x}w_{n}^{J}(\tau_{n},x)\cdot\nabla_{t,x}U_{n}^{j_{0}}(\tau_{n},x)dx+\int_{|x|>\rho_{n}}\nabla\varepsilon_{n}^{J}(\tau_{n},x)\cdot\nabla_{t,x}U_{n}^{j_{0}}(\tau_{n},x)dx\\
=\int_{|x|>\rho_{n}}\left(\nabla_{t,x}u(t_{0},x)-\nabla_{t,x}v(t_{0},x)\right)\cdot\nabla_{t,x}U_{n}^{j_{0}}(\tau_{n},x)dx\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}0,

where we have used that
\lim\tau_{n}=1-t_{0}
, and that
\vec{u}(t_{0},x)-\vec{v}(t_{0},x)=0
for
|x|>1-t_{0}
.
Since (
6.22
) holds for all
t\leq 0
, we have

(6.31)

\forall n,\quad\int_{|x|>\rho_{n}}|\nabla U_{n}^{j_{0}}(\tau_{n},x)|^{2}dx\geq\eta_{1}.

By Lemma
6.2
,

(6.32)

\displaystyle j\neq j_{0}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{|x|>\rho_{n}}\nabla_{t,x}U_{n}^{j}(\tau_{n},x)\cdot\nabla_{t,x}U_{n}^{j_{0}}(\tau_{n},x)\,dx=0

(6.33)

\displaystyle J\geq j_{0}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{|x|>\rho_{n}}\nabla_{t,x}w_{n}^{J}(\tau_{n},x)\cdot\nabla_{t,x}U_{n}^{j_{0}}(\tau_{n},x)\,dx=0.

Combining (
6.24
), (
6.30
), (
6.31
), (
6.32
) and (
6.33
) we obtain a contradiction.

Step 4. Scattering profiles.
We next prove that
\mathcal{I}_{+}
and
\mathcal{I}_{-}
are empty. The proof is close to the proof of the fact that
\mathcal{I}_{c}
is empty, and we only sketch it, focusing on the arguments that are new with respect to Step 3. By Step 3,
\mathcal{J}\subset\mathcal{I}_{c}=\emptyset
, and thus (
6.25
) and (
6.24
) hold with
R_{n}=0
.

Assume that
\mathcal{I}_{+}\neq\emptyset
, and let
j_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{+}
. Recall that
U^{j_{0}}
is a nonzero solution of the linear wave equation. By Proposition
A.1
and Remark
A.2
, we obtain that there exist
A\in\mathbb{R}
,
\eta_{1}>0
and
T
such that

\forall t\geq T,\quad\int_{|x|>t+A}|\nabla_{t,x}U^{j_{0}}(t,x)|^{2}dx\geq\eta_{1}>0.

Changing variables, we deduce

\forall\tau\geq\lambda_{j_{0},n}T+t_{j_{0},n},\quad\int_{|y|>\tau-t_{j_{0},n}+\lambda_{j_{0},n}A}|\nabla_{\tau,y}U_{n}^{j_{0}}(\tau,y)|^{2}dy\geq\eta_{1}.

We will use the preceding bound with
\tau=1-t_{n}
, which is possible since
-t_{j_{0},n}/\lambda_{j_{0},n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
. Indeed,
\lambda_{j_{0},n}T+t_{j_{0},n}<0
, and thus
1-t_{n}\geq\lambda_{j_{0},n}T+t_{j_{0},n}
for large
n
. Denoting
\rho_{n}=1-t_{n}-t_{j_{0},n}+\lambda_{j_{0},n}A
, we obtain that for large
n

\int_{|y|>\rho_{n}}|\nabla_{t,y}U_{n}^{j_{0}}(1-t_{n},y)|^{2}dy\geq\eta_{1}.

We note that
\rho_{n}>1-t_{n}
for large
n
. The end of the proof is the same as the one of Case 1 of Step 3 above, and we omit it.

The proof of the fact that
\mathcal{I}_{-}=\emptyset
is along the same lines (following Case 2 of Step 3) and is also omitted.

Step 5.
We have proved the existence of
J\geq 0
,
\{\iota_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq J}\in\{\pm 1\}^{J}
,
0<\lambda_{1,n}\ll\ldots\ll\lambda_{J,n}
such that

(6.34)

\vec{u}(t_{n})=\vec{v}(t_{n})+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\left(\frac{\iota_{j}}{\lambda_{j,n}}W\left(\frac{\cdot}{\lambda_{j,n}}\right),0\right)+(w_{0,n},w_{1,n}),

where, denoting by
w_{n}
the solution of the linear wave equation with initial data
(w_{0,n},w_{1,n})
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\|w_{n}\|_{L^{3}(\mathbb{R},L^{6})}=0.

Using that the support of
u-v
is included in
\{r<1-t\}
, that, by (
6.34
),

\lambda_{J,n}(u-v)(t_{n},\lambda_{J,n}\cdot)\xrightharpoonup[n\to\infty]{}\iota_{J}W,

and that
W
is not compactly supported, we obtain

(6.35)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\lambda_{J,n}}{1-t_{n}}=0.

In this step, we prove that

(6.36)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\|w_{0,n}\|_{\dot{H}^{1}}=0,

which will conclude the proof of Proposition
6.4
. By (
3.4
) and the time decay of the exterior energy for the linear wave equation, the following holds for all
t\geq 0
or for all
t\leq 0
:

(6.37)

C\int_{|x|>|t|}|\nabla_{t,x}w_{n}(t,x)|^{2}dx\geq\|w_{0,n}\|_{\dot{H}^{1}}^{2}.

On the other hand, since by (
6.34
),

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{|x|\geq 1-t_{n}}\left(|\nabla w_{0,n}(x)|^{2}+w_{1,n}^{2}(x)\right)dx=0,

we see that for any
\varepsilon>0
, if
n
is large enough,

\forall t,\quad\int_{|x|\geq 1-t_{n}+|t|}|\nabla_{t,x}w_{n}(t,x)|^{2}dx\leq\varepsilon.

Thus for large
n
, the following holds for all
t\geq 0
or for all
t\leq 0
.

(6.38)

C\int_{|t|<|x|<|t|+(1-t_{n})}|\nabla_{t,x}w_{n}(t,x)|^{2}dx\geq\|w_{0,n}\|_{\dot{H}^{1}}^{2}.

Arguing by contradiction along the same lines as in Step 3, we obtain (
6.36
). This and (
6.34
) give (
6.13
) and (
6.14
).
∎

6.2.3.
End of the proof

We are now in position to prove Theorem
4
in the finite time blow-up case.
We consider
u
as above, and let
(v_{0},v_{1})
be given by Lemma
6.3
.

Step 1. Boundedness.
We first prove that
u
remains bounded in
\mathcal{H}
as
t\to T_{+}
. For this, we let
\{t_{n}\}_{n}\to T_{+}
be a sequence of times such that
\{\vec{u}(t_{n})\}_{n}
is bounded in
\mathcal{H}
. By Proposition
6.4
, there exists
J
such that

(6.39)

\displaystyle\|u(t_{n})\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{1}}=\|v_{0}\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{1}}+J\|W\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{1}}+o_{n}(1)

(6.40)

\displaystyle E(u_{0},u_{1})=E(v_{0},v_{1})+JE(W,0)+\frac{1}{2}\left\|\partial_{t}u(t_{n})-v_{1}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+o_{n}(1).

From (
6.40
), we deduce

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left\|\partial_{t}u(t_{n})-v_{1}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq E(u_{0},u_{1})-E(v_{0},v_{1})

and

J\leq\frac{E(u_{0},u_{1})-E(v_{0},v_{1})}{E(W,0)}.

From the equation
-\Delta W=W^{3}
, we obtain
\int|\nabla W|^{2}=\int|W|^{4}
and thus
\|W\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{1}}=4E(W,0)
. Using also (
6.39
), we deduce

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|u(t_{n})\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{1}}\leq\|v_{0}\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{1}}+4(E(u_{0},u_{1})-E(v_{0},v_{1})).

Combining, we see that there exists a constant
C_{0}
, depending only on
u
(but independent of the choice of the sequence
\{t_{n}\}_{n}
) such that if
\{\vec{u}(t_{n})\}_{n}
is bounded in
\mathcal{H}
,

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\vec{u}(t_{n})\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq C_{0}.

Using that

\liminf_{t\to\infty}\|\vec{u}(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}}<\infty

we deduce immediately, since
t\mapsto\|\vec{u}(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}}
is continuous,

\limsup_{t\to T_{+}}\|\vec{u}(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq C_{0}.

Step 2. Convergence to
0
of the time derivative inside the wave cone.
In this step we prove

(6.41)

\lim_{t\to T_{+}}\|\partial_{t}u(t)-v_{1}\|_{L^{2}}=0.

By Step 1 and the soliton resolution for a sequence of times proved in
[
10
]
, there exists a sequence
\{t_{n}\}_{n}
,
J\geq 1
,
(\iota_{j})_{j}\in\{\pm 1\}^{J}
, sequences
\{\lambda_{j,n}\}_{n}
,
1\leq j\leq J
with

(6.42)

\lambda_{1,n}\ll\ldots\ll\lambda_{J,n}\ll T_{+}-t_{n},

such that

(6.43)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|\vec{u}(t_{n})-(v_{0},v_{1})-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\left(\frac{\iota_{j}}{\lambda_{j,n}}W\left(\frac{\cdot}{\lambda_{j,n}}\right),0\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}=0.

Note that the conclusion of Step 1 is necessary here, since the main result of
[
10
]
is valid assuming that
\vec{u}(t)
is bounded in
\mathcal{H}
, a stronger statement than our assumption (
6.12
).

As an immediate consequence of this, we obtain

(6.44)

E(u_{0},u_{1})=E(v_{0},v_{1})+JE(W,0).

We prove (
6.41
) by contradiction. If it does not hold, using (
6.43
), we can find an arbitrarily small
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and a sequence
t_{n}^{\prime}\to T_{+}
such that
\|\partial_{t}u(t_{n}^{\prime})-v_{1}\|_{L^{2}}=\varepsilon_{0}
. By Proposition
6.4
, there exists an integer
J^{\prime}\geq 0
such that

E(u_{0},u_{1})=E(v_{0},v_{1})+J^{\prime}E(W,0)+\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{0}^{2}.

Combining with (
6.44
), we deduce that

\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{0}^{2}=(J-J^{\prime})E(W,0),

a contradiction if
\varepsilon_{0}^{2}
is smaller than
E(W,0)
.

Step 3. End of the proof.
Combining Steps
1
and
2
with Proposition
6.4
, we see that for any sequence
\{t_{n}\}_{n}\to T_{+}
, extracting subsequences if necessary, there exist
J\geq 1
,
(\iota_{j})_{j}\in\{\pm 1\}^{J}
, sequences
\{\lambda_{j,n}\}_{n}
,
1\leq j\leq J
such that (
6.42
) and (
6.43
) hold. We note that

J=\frac{E(u_{0},u_{1})-E(v_{0},v_{1})}{E(W,0)}

is independent of the choice of
\{t_{n}\}_{n}
. The case
J=0
is excluded since
T_{+}
is the maximal time of existence of
u
.

It remains to construct the scaling parameters
\lambda_{j}(t)
such that the expansion (
1.20
) holds.
This can be done as in
[
20
]
, Section 3.5
defining, for
j=1\ldots J
and
t<1
close to
1
,

B_{j}:=(j-1)\|\nabla W\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{|x|\leq 1}|\nabla W(x)|^{2}\,dx

and

\lambda_{j}(t):=\inf\left\{\lambda>0\text{ s.t. }\int_{|x|\leq\lambda}\left|\nabla(u-v)(t,x)\right|^{2}\,dx\geq B_{j}\right\}.

We refer to Section 3.5 of
[
20
]
for the proof that the conclusion of Theorem
4
in the finite time blow-up case holds with this choice of
\lambda_{j}(t)

6.3.
Comments on the proof in the global case

We recall:

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
u
be a solution of (
1.7
) such that
T_{+}(u)=+\infty
. Then

•

\displaystyle\liminf_{t\to\infty}\|\vec{u}(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}}<\infty
.

•

There exists a finite energy solution
v_{L}
of the free wave equation such that

\forall A\in\mathbb{R},\quad\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{|x|>t+A}|\nabla_{t,x}(u-v_{L})(t,x)|^{2}dx=0.

See Subsections 3.2 and 3.3 in
[
20
]
for the proofs. The proofs are written in space dimension
3
there, but are indeed independent of the dimension. See also
[
10
]
, Subsection 4.1, for the proof of the second point in space dimension
4
.

In view of Proposition
6.5
, the proof of Theorem
4
in the global case is very close to the corresponding proof in the finite time blow-up case, replacing
(v_{0},v_{1})
and
t=1
by
\vec{v}_{L}(t)
and
t=+\infty
, and we omit it.

7.
Soliton resolution for corotional wave maps

7.1.
Preliminaries on wave maps

7.1.1.
Miscellaneous results on wave maps

We gather here a few standard results on wave maps.

Claim 7.1
.

Let
(\psi_{0},\psi_{1})\in\mathbf{E}
, such that
E_{M}(\psi_{0},\psi_{1})
is small. Then there exists
\ell
such that
(\psi_{0},\psi_{1})\in\mathbf{H}_{\ell,\ell}
and

\|(\psi_{0}-\ell\pi,\psi_{1})\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\approx E_{M}(\psi_{0},\psi_{1}).

Similarly, if
R>0
,
(\psi_{0}-\ell\pi,\psi_{1})\in\mathbf{H}(R)
for some
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
, and

\int_{R}^{\infty}\left(\psi_{1}^{2}+(\partial_{r}\psi_{0})^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\sin^{2}\psi_{0}(r)\right)rdr

is small, then

\int_{R}^{\infty}\left(\psi_{1}^{2}+(\partial_{r}\psi_{0})^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\sin^{2}\psi_{0}(r)\right)rdr\\
\approx\int_{R}^{\infty}\left(\psi_{1}^{2}+(\partial_{r}\psi_{0})^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}(\psi_{0}(r)-\ell\pi)^{2}\right)rdr.

Proof.

(See also
[
45
, Lemma 5.3]
). Let
(\psi_{0},\psi_{1})\in\mathbf{E}
, and
(\ell,m)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
such that
(\psi_{0},\psi_{1})\in\mathbf{H}_{\ell,m}
. Since
\psi_{0}(0)=\ell\pi
, we have, for any
r>0
,

\left|\int_{\ell\pi}^{\psi_{0}(r)}|\sin(\sigma)|d\sigma\right|\leq\int_{0}^{r}|\sin(\psi_{0}(\sigma))|\,|\partial_{r}\psi_{0}(\sigma)|d\sigma\leq E_{M}(\psi_{0},\psi_{1}).

Since
E_{M}(\psi_{0},\psi_{1})
is small, we deduce that
|\psi_{0}(r)-\ell\pi|
is small for all
r
. This implies in particular
\psi_{0}\in\mathbf{H}_{\ell,\ell}
,
\frac{1}{2}\left|\psi_{0}(r)-\ell\pi\right|\leq|\sin(\psi_{0}(r))|\leq|\psi_{0}(r)-\ell\pi|
and the first assertion of the claim follows. The proof of the second assertion is similar and we omit it.
∎

Recalling that the outer energy

E_{\out}(\vec{\psi}(t))=\frac{1}{2}\int_{|t|}^{\infty}\left((\partial_{t}\psi(t,r))^{2}+(\partial_{r}\psi(t,r))^{2}+\frac{\sin^{2}\psi(t,r)}{r^{2}}\right)rdr

is nonincreasing for
t>0
(and nondecreasing for
t<0
), we can now complete the proof of Theorem
1
.

End of the proof of the rigidity theorem.

Let
\psi
be a solution of (
1.1
) which is not a stationary solution, with data
(\psi_{0},\psi_{1})\in\mathbf{H}_{\ell,m}
. We have already proved (
4.8
). Assume to fix ideas that

(7.1)

\lim_{t\to+\infty}\int_{|t|}^{\infty}\left((\partial_{t,r}\psi(t,r))^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}(\psi(t,r)-m\pi)^{2}\right)rdr>0.

Let

\eta=\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{t}^{\infty}\left((\partial_{t,r}\psi(t,r))^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\sin^{2}\psi(t,r)\right)rdr=\lim_{t\to\infty}E_{\out}(\vec{\psi}(t)).

Since
E_{\out}
is nonincreasing for
t\geq 0
, we are reduced to prove that
\eta>0
. However if
\eta=0
, then for large
t
,
E_{\out}(\vec{\psi}(t))
is small and the second assertion of Claim
7.1
applies. This yields a contradiction with (
7.1
), concluding the proof of (
1.13
).
∎

We will need the following estimates on the sinus functions:

Claim 7.2
.

Let
J\geq 2
,
(a_{j})_{1\leq j\leq J}\in\mathbb{R}^{J}
. Then

(7.2)

\displaystyle\left|\sin\Big(2\sum_{j=1}^{J}a_{j}\Big)-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sin(2a_{j})\right|\lesssim\sum_{j\neq k}|\sin(2a_{j})|\,\sin^{2}(a_{k})\lesssim\sum_{j\neq k}|\sin(a_{j})|\,\sin^{2}(a_{k})

(7.3)

\displaystyle\left|\sin^{2}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{J}a_{j}\Big)-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sin^{2}(a_{j})\right|\lesssim\sum_{j\neq k}\left|\sin a_{j}\sin a_{k}\right|

Proof.

Proof of (
7.2
).
The inequality (
7.2
) with
J=2
follows from

\sin(2(a_{1}+a_{2}))-\sin(2a_{1})-\sin(2a_{2})=-2\sin(2a_{1})\sin^{2}a_{2}-2\sin(2a_{2})\sin^{2}a_{1}.

The general case follows from a straightforward induction and the bound
|\sin(a+b)|\leq|\sin a|+[\sin b|
.

Proof of (
7.3
).
In the case
J=2
, we have

\left|\sin^{2}(a_{1}+a_{2})-\sin^{2}a_{1}-\sin^{2}a_{2}\right|=2\left|\sin a_{1}\sin a_{2}\cos(a_{1}-a_{2})\right|\leq 2|\sin a_{1}\sin a_{2}|.

The general case follows from an elementary induction.
∎

In the following proposition, we gather some important facts about finite time blow-up solutions of (
1.1
)

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
\psi
be a finite energy solution of (
1.1
) with maximal time of existence
T_{+}<\infty
. Then there exists
(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1})\in\mathbf{E}
such that for all
R>0
,

\lim_{t\to T_{+}}\int_{R}^{\infty}\left((\partial_{t}\psi(t)-\varphi_{1})^{2}+(\partial_{r}\psi(t)-\varphi_{0})^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}(\psi(t)-\varphi_{0})^{2}\right)r\,dr=0.

The solution of (
1.1
), with initial data
(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1})
at
t=T_{+}
satisfies

\psi(t,r)=\varphi(t,r),\quad 0<r<T_{+}-t,\quad t<T_{+}\text{ close to }T_{+}.

Furthermore,

(7.4)

\lim_{t\to T_{+}}\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T_{+}-t}\left((\partial_{t}\psi(t,r))^{2}+(\partial_{r}\psi(t,r))^{2}+\frac{\sin^{2}(r)}{r^{2}}\right)rdr\geq E_{M}(Q,0).

Finally, there exists
t_{n}\to T_{+}
such that

(7.5)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T_{+}-t_{n}}(\partial_{t}\psi(t_{n},r)^{2}rdr=0.

Note that the energy inside the wave cone

E_{\inn}(\vec{\psi}(t))=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T_{+}-t}\left((\partial_{t}\psi(t,r))^{2}+(\partial_{r}\psi(t,r))^{2}+\frac{\sin^{2}(r)}{r^{2}}\right)rdr

is a nonincreasing function of
t\in[0,T_{+})
