, so that the limit appearing in (
7.4
) exists.

The existence of
(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1})
follows from small data well-posedness arguments and finite speed of propagation. See
[
8
, Lemma 5.2]
,
[
7
, Proposition 5.2]
. The lower bound (
7.4
) is an immediate consequence of the work of Struwe
[
78
]
. Finally, (
7.5
) can be seen as a consequence of the main result of
[
7
]
, but is indeed a step of the proof of this main result (see
[
7
, Corollary 2.7]
).

Recall the linearized equation for (
1.1
) around a constant solution:

(7.6)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}^{2}\psi_{L}-\partial_{r}^{2}\psi_{L}-\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}\psi_{L}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\psi_{L}=0\\
\vec{\psi}_{L\restriction t=0}=(\psi_{0},\psi_{1})\in\mathbf{H}.\end{aligned}\right.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
\psi
be a solution of (
1.1
) with maximal time of existence
T_{+}=+\infty
and initial data
(\psi_{0},\psi_{1})\in\mathbf{H}_{\ell,m}
. Then there exists a solution
\psi_{L}
of (
7.6
) with initial data in
\mathbf{H}
and an increasing positive function
\alpha(t)=o(t)
as
t\to\infty
such that

(7.7)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\left\|(\psi(t)-m\pi-\psi_{L}(t),\partial_{t}\psi(t)-\partial_{t}\psi_{L}(t))\right\|_{\mathbf{H}(\alpha(t))}=0.

Furthermore, there exists
t_{n}\to+\infty
such that

(7.8)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}(\partial_{t}\psi(t_{n},r)-\partial_{t}\psi_{L}(t_{n},r))^{2}rdr=0.

See
[
7
, Proposition 5.1]
for (
7.7
) and
[
7
, Corollary 2.3]
for (
7.8
).

7.1.2.
Rigidity theorem

7.1.3.
A space-time bound outside the wave cone

We will denote by
S
the space of measurable functions

\psi
of
(t,r)\in\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)
such that the following norm is finite.

(7.9)

\|\psi\|_{S}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|\psi(t,r)|^{6}\frac{dr}{r^{3}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}dt\right)^{\frac{1}{3}},

and by
S(\{r>|t|\}
the space of restrictions of
S
to
\{r>|t|\}
, with the norm

(7.10)

\|\psi\|_{S(\{r>[t[\})}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{|t|}^{\infty}|\psi(t,r)|^{6}\frac{dr}{r^{3}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}dt\right)^{\frac{1}{3}},

By the change of function
u=\frac{1}{r}\psi
and the Strichartz estimate for the wave equation in dimension
1+4
, all solutions of (
7.6
) are in
S
. This is of course not the case for general finite energy solution of the wave map equation. However we have the following space time bound, which will be useful to construct a nonlinear profile decomposition for (
1.1
) outside wave cones.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
(\psi_{0},\psi_{1})\in\mathbf{H}_{\ell,m}
,
(\ell,m)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, and let
\psi
be the solution of (
1.1
) with initial data
(\psi_{0},\psi_{1})
. Then
\sin\psi\in S(\{r>|t|\})
, i.e.

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(\int_{|t|}^{\infty}\frac{\sin^{6}\psi(t,r)}{r^{3}}dr\right)^{1/2}dt<\infty.

Remark 7.6
.

The solution
\psi
is well-defined for
r>|t|
, see Claim
2.1
.

Proof.

Let
u=\frac{\psi-\ell\pi}{r}
,
u_{0}=\frac{(\psi_{0}-\ell\pi)}{r}
,
u_{1}=\frac{\psi_{1}}{r}
. Since
(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1})\in\mathbf{H}_{\ell,m}
,
(u_{0},u_{1})
can be considered as a radial function in
(\dot{H}^{1}\times L^{2})_{\loc}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
. By the local well-posedness for the equation (
4.1
) and finite speed of propagation,
u
is defined on
\{-t_{0}\leq t\leq t_{0},\;0<r\leq 2\}
for some small
t_{0}>0
, and

\int_{-t_{0}}^{t_{0}}\left(\int_{0}^{2}u^{6}(t,r)r^{3}dr\right)^{1/2}dt<\infty.

Hence

(7.11)

\int_{-t_{0}}^{t_{0}}\left(\int_{|t|}^{2}\frac{(\sin{\psi})^{6}}{r^{3}}dr\right)^{1/2}dt\leq\int_{-t_{0}}^{t_{0}}\left(\int_{|t|}^{2}\frac{(\psi-\ell\pi)^{6}(t,r)}{r^{3}}dr\right)^{1/2}dt<\infty.

Similarly, for all
R>0
,
\tilde{u}=\frac{\psi-m\pi}{r}
is a solution of (
4.1
) defined for
r>R+|t|
with initial data in
\mathcal{H}(R)
. Thus for all
T>0
,

\int_{-T}^{T}\left(\int_{R+|t|}^{\infty}{\tilde{u}}^{6}(t,r)r^{3}dr\right)^{1/2}dt<\infty,

which yields

\int_{-T}^{T}\left(\int_{R+|t|}^{\infty}\frac{(\sin\psi)^{6}}{r^{3}}dr\right)^{1/2}dt<\infty.

Combining with a space translation in time and (
7.11
) we obtain

(7.12)

\forall T>0,\quad\int_{-T}^{T}\left(\int_{|t|}^{\infty}\frac{(\sin\psi)^{6}}{r^{3}}dr\right)^{1/2}dt<\infty

Using that
E_{\out}
is nonincreasing for
t>0
, ¡e define

(7.13)

E_{\out}(\infty)=\lim_{t\to+\infty}E_{\out}\big(\vec{\psi}(t)\big).

Let
T
such that

(7.14)

E_{\out}\big(\vec{\psi}(T)\big)\leq E_{\out}(\infty)+\frac{1}{4}E_{M}(Q,0).

We extend
\vec{\psi}(T,r)
, which is defined for
r>T
, as follows. We define

(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})(r)=(\psi(T,r),\partial_{t}\psi(T,r)),\quad r\geq T,

and
\varphi_{1}(r)=0
if
r<T
. Let
p\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
p\pi\leq\psi(T,T)<(p+1)\pi
. For
r<T
, we let

\begin{cases}\phi_{0}(r)=p\pi&\text{ if }\psi(T,T)=p\pi\\
\phi_{0}(r)=Q(\lambda r)+p\pi&\text{ if }p\pi<\psi(T,T)\leq p\pi+\pi/2\\
\phi_{0}(r)=(p+1)\pi-Q(\lambda r)&\text{ if }p\pi+\pi/2<\psi(T,T)<(p+1)\pi,\end{cases}

where in the second and third case,
\lambda
is chosen so that
\phi_{0}
is continuous at
r=T
. We note that
(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})\in\mathbf{E}
. We claim

(7.15)

E_{M}(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})\leq E_{\out}(\infty)+\frac{3}{4}E_{M}(Q,0).

In view of (
7.14
), it is sufficient to check that

(7.16)

\int_{0}^{T}(\partial_{r}\phi_{0})^{2}rdr+\int_{0}^{T}\sin^{2}\phi_{0}\frac{dr}{r}\leq E_{M}(Q,0).

In the case where
\phi_{0}(r)=p\pi
for
r<T
, this is trivial. In the two other cases, it follows from the fact that
Q(1)=2\arctan 1=\pi/2
and that

\int_{0}^{1}(\partial_{r}Q)^{2}rdr+\int_{0}^{1}\sin^{2}Q\frac{dr}{r}=E_{M}(Q,0)

which can be proved by an explicit computation.

Let
\phi
be the solution of (
1.1
) with initial data
\vec{\phi}(T)=(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})
. By finite speed of propagation,
\psi(t,r)=\phi(t,r)
,
r>t\geq T
. As a consequence, for
t\geq T
,
E_{\out}(\vec{\phi}(t))\geq E_{\out}(\infty)
. Combining with the conservation of the energy and (
7.15
), we deduce

\forall t\geq T,\quad(E_{M}-E_{\out})(\vec{\phi}(t))\leq\frac{3}{4}E_{M}(Q,0).

In view of the energy concentration for blow-up solutions (see (
7.4
)), the solution
\phi
is global in the future. Let
\phi_{L}
be the solution of (
7.6
) given by Proposition
7.4
, so that

(7.17)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\left\|(\phi(t)-m\pi-\phi_{L}(t),\partial_{t}\phi(t)-\partial_{t}\phi_{L}(t))\right\|_{\mathbf{H}(\alpha(t))}=0,

where
\alpha(t)/t\to 0
as
t\to\infty
.
Let
\widetilde{T}\gg 1
, so that

\left\|\left(\phi\big(\widetilde{T}\big)-m\pi-\phi_{L}\big(\widetilde{T}\big),\partial_{t}\phi\big(\widetilde{T}\big)-\partial_{t}\phi_{L}\big(\widetilde{T}\big)\right)\right\|_{\mathbf{H}(\widetilde{T})}+\int_{\widetilde{T}}^{\infty}\left(\int_{|t|}^{\infty}\frac{\phi^{6}_{L}(t,r)}{r^{3}}dr\right)^{1/2}dt\ll 1.

Recall that
\frac{1}{r}\phi_{L}
is a solution of the linear wave equation in
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{4}
, and that
\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{3}r}(\phi-m\pi)
is solution of the nonlinear equation (
4.1
). Then by Proposition
4.1
,

\int_{\widetilde{T}}^{\infty}\left(\int_{|t|}^{\infty}\frac{\left(\phi(t)-m\pi\right)^{6}}{r^{3}}dr\right)^{1/2}dt<\infty,

which implies

\int_{\widetilde{T}}^{\infty}\left(\int_{|t|}^{\infty}\frac{\sin^{6}\psi(t,r)}{r^{3}}dr\right)^{1/2}dt=\int_{\widetilde{T}}^{\infty}\left(\int_{|t|}^{\infty}\frac{\sin^{6}\phi(t,r)}{r^{3}}dr\right)^{1/2}dt<\infty.

Using the same argument for negative times, we obtain the desired conclusion.
∎

7.1.4.
Profile decomposition

We next state a profile decomposition which is adapted to the wave maps equation (
1.1
).

Proposition 7.7

(Profile decomposition for wave maps)
.

Let
(\ell,m)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Consider a sequence
\left\{(\psi_{0,n},\psi_{1,n})\right\}_{n}
of elements of
\mathbf{H}_{\ell,m}
such that

(7.18)

\sup_{n}E_{M}(\psi_{0,n},\psi_{1,n})<\infty.

Then there exists a subsequence of
\left\{(\psi_{0,n},\psi_{1,n})\right\}_{n}
(that we denote the same) and, for all
j\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
, for all
n
,
\left(\Psi_{0,n}^{j},\Psi_{1,n}^{j}\right)\in\mathbf{E}
,
\lambda_{j,n}>0
,
t_{j,n}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

(7.19)

j\neq k\Longrightarrow\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\lambda_{j,n}}{\lambda_{k,n}}+\frac{\lambda_{k,n}}{\lambda_{j,n}}+\frac{|t_{j,n}-t_{k,n}|}{\lambda_{j,n}}=+\infty,

and, for all
j\geq 1
, one the following holds:

Compact profile:

\forall n
,
t_{j,n}=0
and

\exists\left(\Psi_{0}^{j},\Psi_{1}^{j}\right)\in\mathbf{E},\quad(\Psi_{0,n}^{j}(r),\Psi_{1,n}^{j}(r))=\left(\Psi_{0}^{j}\left(\frac{r}{\lambda_{j,n}}\right),\frac{1}{\lambda_{j,n}}\Psi_{1}^{j}\left(\frac{r}{\lambda_{j,n}}\right)\right).

Linear wave profile:

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{-t_{j,n}}{\lambda_{j,n}}\in\{\pm\infty\}
and there exists a solution
\Psi^{j}_{L}
of (
7.6
) with initial data
(\Psi_{0}^{j},\Psi_{1}^{j})\in\mathbf{H}
such that

(\Psi_{0,n}^{j}(r),\Psi_{1,n}^{j}(r))=\left(\Psi_{L}^{j}\left(\frac{-t_{j,n}}{\lambda_{j,n}},\frac{r}{\lambda_{j,n}}\right),\frac{1}{\lambda_{j,n}}\Psi_{L}^{j}\left(\frac{-t_{j,n}}{\lambda_{j,n}},\frac{r}{\lambda_{j,n}}\right)\right).

Furthermore, denoting by

(7.20)

\left(\omega_{0,n}^{J},\omega_{1,n}^{J}\right)=\left(\psi_{0,n},\psi_{1,n}\right)-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\left(\Psi_{0,n}^{j},\Psi_{1,n}^{j}\right),

then for large
J
,
(\omega_{0,n}^{J},\omega_{1,n}^{J})\in\mathbf{H}
for all
n
,

(7.21)

E_{M}\left(\psi_{0,n},\psi_{1,n}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{J}E_{M}\left(\Psi_{0,n}^{J},\Psi_{1,n}^{J}\right)+E_{M}\left(\omega_{0,n}^{J},\omega_{1,n}^{J}\right)+o_{n}(1),

and, letting
\omega_{n}^{J}
be the solution of (
7.6
) with initial data
(\omega_{0,n}^{J},\omega_{1,n}^{J})
, one has

(7.22)

\lim_{J\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left\|\omega_{n}^{J}\right\|_{S}+\|\omega_{n}^{J}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t,r}}=0.

Finally, for large
J
,
\left\{(\omega_{0,n}^{J},\omega_{1,n}^{J})\right\}_{n}
is bounded in
\mathbf{H}
and

(7.23)

1\leq j\leq J\Longrightarrow\left(\omega_{n}^{J}(t_{j,n},\lambda_{j,n}\cdot),\lambda_{j,n}\partial_{t}\omega_{n}^{J}(t_{j,n},\lambda_{j,n}\cdot)\right)\xrightharpoonup[n\to\infty]{}0\text{ in }\mathbf{H}.

We will denote by
\mathcal{J}_{C}
the indices corresponding to compact profiles, and by
\mathcal{J}_{L}
the indices corresponding to nonzero linear wave profiles. We can assume that there exists at most one nonzero constant profile (which is a compact profile). We will consider the profiles such that
(\Psi_{0}^{j},\Psi_{1}^{j})\equiv 0
, as compact profiles. Of course, if there is an infinite number of nonzero profiles, we can assume (rearranging the indices if necessary) that there are no such profiles.

By (
7.21
),

\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}_{C}}E_{M}(\Psi_{0}^{j},\Psi_{1}^{j})<\infty.

By Claim
7.1
, for large
j
,
(\Psi_{0}^{j},\Psi_{1}^{j})\in\mathbf{H}_{\ell_{j},\ell_{j}}
for some
\ell_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}
, and, since
(\omega_{0,n}^{J},\omega_{1,n}^{J})\in\mathbf{H}
for large
J
,
(\Psi_{0}^{j},\Psi_{1}^{j})\in\mathbf{H}
for large
j
.

Proposition
7.7
is
[
45
, Lemma 5.5]
, with some changes in the notations:

•

The constants denoted by
\phi_{0}^{l}(0)
that appear in the decomposition (5.16) of
[
45
]
can be summed up all together and considered (if this sum is not zero) as a constant profile
(\Psi_{0}^{1},\Psi_{1}^{1})=(p\pi,0)
,
p\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
with
1\in\mathcal{J}_{C}
.

•

In
[
45
, Lemma 5.5]
the solutions of (
7.6
) are denoted by
ru_{L}
where
u_{L}
is a radial solution of the
1+4
-dimensional wave equation.

•

In
[
45
]
, the profiles corresponding to indices
j\in\mathcal{J}_{C}
are divided into two types of profiles, the ones defined as rescaled
(\psi_{0}^{l},0)\in\mathbf{E}
, and the ones defined as rescaled
U^{j}_{L}
, with
t_{j,n}=0
for all
n
(see (5.16) in this article).

The only part of Proposition
7.7
which is not included in
[
45
]
is the Pythagorean expansion (
7.21
) of the energy for wave maps. The proof is given in Appendix
B.2
. We refer to
[
8
, Lemma 2.16]
for a proof of this Pythagorean expansion in a special case.

We now state the analog of Proposition
6.1
(a nonlinear profile decomposition outside a wave cone) for wave maps. Since the solutions of (
1.1
) are always defined on the entire exterior of a wave cone
\{r>|t|\}
and satisfy the space-time bound given by Lemma
7.5
there, this decomposition will be valid on
\{r>|t|\}
.

Let
(\psi_{0,n},\psi_{1,n})
, and, for
j\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
,
\left(\Psi_{0}^{j},\Psi_{1}^{j}\right)
,
\{t_{j,n}\}_{n}
,
\{\lambda_{j,n}\}_{n}
,
\omega_{n}^{j}
be as in Proposition
7.7
. If
j\in\mathcal{J}_{C}
, we denote by
\Psi^{j}
the solution of (
1.1
) with initial data
\left(\Psi_{0}^{j},\Psi_{1}^{j}\right)
at
t=0
, which is well-defined for
r>|t|
(see Claim
2.1
). If
j\in\mathcal{J}_{L}
, we will denote
\Psi^{j}=\Psi^{j}_{L}
. Let

(7.24)

\Psi_{n}^{j}(t,r)=\Psi^{j}\left(\frac{t-t_{j,n}}{\lambda_{j,n}},\frac{r}{\lambda_{j,n}}\right).

Proposition 7.8
.

Let
\psi_{n}
be the solution of (
1.1
) with initial data
(\psi_{0,n},\psi_{1,n})
. Then, denoting,

(7.25)

\Theta_{n}^{J}(t,r)=\psi_{n}(t,r)-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\Psi_{n}^{j}(t,r)-\omega_{n}^{J}(t,r),\quad r>|t|,

one has for large
J
that
\vec{\Theta}_{n}^{J}(t)\in\mathbf{H}(|t|)
for all
t
and

\lim_{J\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left(\sup_{t}\left\|\vec{\Theta}_{n}^{J}(t)\right\|_{\mathbf{H}(|t|)}+\left\|\Theta_{n}^{J}\right\|_{S(\{r>|t|\})}\right)=0.

Proof.

If we assume that the sequence
\left\{(\psi_{0,n},\psi_{1,n})\right\}_{n}
is bounded in
\mathbf{H}
, then the conclusion of the proposition can be obtained by considering the sequence of radial finite-energy solutions
\left\{\frac{1}{r}\psi_{n}\right\}_{n}
of the
1+4-
dimensional nonlinear wave equation (
4.1
) (see e.g.
[
7
, Proposition 2.15]
). To work in the more general setting of Proposition
7.8
, we will need the space-time bound for general finite-energy solutions of (
1.1
) given in Lemma
7.5
.

We denote

(7.26)

\psi_{n}^{J}=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\Psi_{n}^{J}+\omega_{n}^{J},\quad\Theta_{n}^{J}=\psi_{n}-\psi_{n}^{J}.

Then

(7.27)

\partial_{t}^{2}\Theta_{n}^{J}-\partial_{r}^{2}\Theta_{n}^{J}-\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}\Theta_{n}^{J}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Theta_{n}^{J}\\
=-\frac{\sin(2\psi_{n})-2\psi_{n}}{2r^{2}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq J\\
j\in\mathcal{J}_{C}\end{subarray}}\frac{\sin(2\Psi_{n}^{j})-2\Psi_{n}^{j}}{2r^{2}},

with initial data
(\Theta_{n}^{J},\partial_{t}\Theta_{n}^{j})_{\restriction t=0}=(0,0)
. Expanding the second line of (
7.27
) and letting
\theta_{n}^{j}=\frac{1}{r}\Theta_{n}^{j}
, we rewrite it as a radial
1+4
-dimensional wave equation:

(7.28)

\partial_{t}^{2}\theta_{n}^{J}-\partial_{r}^{2}\theta_{n}^{J}-\frac{3}{r}\partial_{r}\theta_{n}^{J}\\
=-\frac{1}{2r^{3}}\left(\sin\bigg(2\omega_{n}^{J}+2\Theta_{n}^{J}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}2\Psi_{n}^{j}\bigg)-\sin\left(2\omega_{n}^{J}\right)-\sin\left(2\Theta_{n}^{J}\right)-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sin\left(2\Psi_{n}^{j}\right)\right)\\
+\frac{1}{r^{3}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq J\\
j\in\mathcal{J}_{L}\end{subarray}}\left(\Psi_{n}^{j}-\frac{\sin(2\Psi_{n}^{j})}{2}\right)+\frac{1}{r^{3}}\left(\Theta_{n}^{J}-\frac{\sin(2\Theta_{n}^{J})}{2}\right)+\frac{1}{r^{3}}\left(\omega_{n}^{J}-\frac{\sin(2\omega_{n}^{J})}{2}\right).

Let
\Gamma_{T}:=\{(t,r)\;:\;0<|t|\leq\min(r,T)\}
. We will prove

(7.29)

\forall T>0,\quad\left\|\left(\partial_{t}^{2}\theta_{n}^{J}-\partial_{r}^{2}\theta_{n}^{J}-\frac{3}{r}\partial_{r}\theta_{n}^{J}\right)1\!\!1_{\Gamma_{T}}\right\|_{L^{1}L^{2}}\\
\leq\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\theta_{n}^{J}(t)\right\|_{L^{6}(|t|)}F_{n}(t)dt+C\int_{0}^{T}\|\theta_{n}^{J}(t)\|^{3}_{L^{6}(|t|)}dt+\varepsilon_{n}^{J},

where
\varepsilon_{n}^{J}
is independent of
T
and is such that

(7.30)

\lim_{J\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\varepsilon_{n}^{J}=0,

and
F_{n}
satisfies

(7.31)

F_{n}\in L^{3/2}(\mathbb{R}),\quad\sup_{n}\|F_{n}\|_{L^{3/2}(\mathbb{R})}<\infty.

In (
7.29
) the notation
L^{6}(|t|)
means as above

\|f\|_{L^{6}(|t|)}=\left(\int_{|t|}^{\infty}\left(f(r)\right)^{6}r^{3}dr\right)^{1/6}.

We first assume (
7.29
), (
7.30
) and (
7.31
) and prove the proposition. In all the sequel we assume that
J
and
n
are taken large enough, so that
\varepsilon_{n}^{J}
is small. By (
7.29
), Strichartz estimates and Hölder’s inequality,

\left(\int_{0}^{T}\|\theta_{n}^{J}(t)\|^{3}_{L^{6}(|t|)}dt\right)^{1/3}\leq\varepsilon_{n}^{J}+\int_{0}^{T}\|\theta_{n}^{J}(t)\|_{L^{6}(|t|)}\left(F_{n}(t)+C\|\theta_{n}^{J}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{6}(|t|)}\right)dt.

Using (
7.31
) and a Grönwall-type inequality (see the appendix of
[
31
]
), we obtain that for all
T>0
,

\left(\int_{0}^{T}\|\theta_{n}^{J}(t)\|_{L^{6}(|t|)}^{3}dt\right)^{1/3}\leq\varepsilon_{n}^{J}\Gamma\left(C+C\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\|\theta_{n}^{J}\|^{3}_{L^{6}(|t|)}dt\Big)^{2/3}\right),

where
\Gamma
is the standard Gamma function.

Assuming
\varepsilon_{n}^{J}\Gamma(C+1)\leq 1/C
, we obtain that

\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\|\theta_{n}^{J}\|^{3}_{L^{6}(|t|)}dt\Big)^{2/3}\leq 1/C\\
\Longrightarrow\left(\int_{0}^{T}\|\theta_{n}^{J}(t)\|_{L^{6}(|t|)}^{3}dt\right)^{1/3}\leq\varepsilon_{n}^{J}\Gamma\left(C+1\right)\leq 1/C,

and a standard bootstrap argument yields

\Big(\int_{0}^{T}\|\theta_{n}^{J}\|^{3}_{L^{6}(|t|)}dt\Big)^{1/3}\lesssim\varepsilon_{n}^{J}
for all
T
. Using (
7.29
) and again Strichartz estimates we see that it implies

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\vec{\theta}_{n}^{J}(t)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}(|t|)}\lesssim\varepsilon_{n}^{J}.

Going back to the function
\Theta_{n}^{j}(t)=r\theta_{n}^{j}
, we deduce the conclusion of the proposition. We are thus left with proving (
7.29
). In view of (
7.28
), it is sufficient to prove the following inequalities

(7.32)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|1\!\!1_{\{r>|t|\}}\frac{1}{r^{3}}\left(\Psi_{n}^{j}-\frac{\sin(2\Psi_{n}^{j})}{2}\right)\right\|_{L^{1}L^{2}}=0,\quad\forall j\in\mathcal{J}_{L}

(7.33)

\displaystyle\lim_{J\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left\|1\!\!1_{\{r>|t|\}}\frac{1}{r^{3}}\left(\omega_{n}^{j}-\frac{\sin(2\omega_{n}^{j})}{2}\right)\right\|_{L^{1}L^{2}}=0

(7.34)

\displaystyle\left\|1\!\!1_{\Gamma_{T}}\frac{1}{r^{3}}\left(\Theta_{n}^{J}-\frac{\sin(2\Theta_{n}^{J})}{2}\right)\right\|_{L^{1}L^{2}}\lesssim\left\|1\!\!1_{\Gamma_{T}}\theta_{n}^{J}\right\|_{L^{3}L^{6}}^{3},\quad\forall J\gg 1

(7.35)

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1\!\!1_{\Gamma_{T}}}{2r^{3}}\left(\sin\bigg(2\omega_{n}^{J}+2\Theta_{n}^{J}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}2\Psi_{n}^{j}\bigg)-\sin\left(2\omega_{n}^{J}\right)-\sin\left(2\Theta_{n}^{J}\right)-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sin\left(2\Psi_{n}^{j}\right)\right)\right\|_{L^{1}L^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\lesssim\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\theta_{n}^{J}(t)\right\|_{L^{6}(|t|)}F_{n}(t)dt+\tilde{\varepsilon}_{n}^{J}+\left\|1\!\!1_{\Gamma_{T}}\theta_{n}^{J}\right\|^{3}_{L^{3}L^{6}},\quad\forall J\gg 1,

where
\tilde{\varepsilon}_{n}^{J}
satisfies (
7.30
),
F_{n}
satisfies (
7.31
), and the space-time
L^{p}L^{q}
norms are always with respect to
r^{3}dr
and
dt
.

Proofs of (
7.32
), (
7.33
) and (
7.34
).
We use the bound

(7.36)

\forall\sigma\in\mathbb{R},\quad\left|\sigma-\frac{\sin(2\sigma)}{2}\right|\lesssim\sigma^{3}.

By (
7.36
) and the property (
7.22
) of
\omega_{n}^{J}
, we obtain (
7.33
).
The inequality (
7.34
) follows directly from (
7.36
) and Hölder’s inequality.

Let
j\in\mathcal{J}_{L}
. Then by (
7.36
) and Hölder’s inequality,

(7.37)

\left\|\frac{1}{r^{3}}\left(\Psi_{n}^{j}-\frac{\sin(2\Psi_{n}^{j})}{2}\right)1\!\!1_{\{r>|t|\}}\right\|_{L^{1}L^{2}}\\
\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{|t|}^{\infty}\frac{1}{r^{3}}\left(\Psi^{j}\left(\frac{t-t_{j,n}}{\lambda_{j,n}},\frac{r}{\lambda_{j,n}}\right)\right)^{6}dr\right)^{1/2}dt\\
=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\left|\frac{t_{j,n}}{\lambda_{j,n}}+\tau\right|}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\rho^{3}}\left(\Psi^{j}(\tau,\rho)\right)^{6}d\rho\right)^{1/2}d\tau,

which tends to
0
as
n\to\infty
by dominated convergence, and since
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{t_{j,n}}{\lambda_{j,n}}+\tau\in\pm\infty
for every
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
if
j\in\mathcal{J}_{L}
. Hence (
7.32
).

Proof of (
7.35
).
By the Claim
7.2
, we have

\left|\sin\bigg(2\omega_{n}^{J}+2\Theta_{n}^{J}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}2\Psi_{n}^{j}\bigg)-\sin\left(2\omega_{n}^{J}\right)-\sin\left(2\Theta_{n}^{J}\right)-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sin\left(2\Psi_{n}^{j}\right)\right|\\
\lesssim\left|\sin\omega_{n}^{J}\right|^{3}+\left|\sin\Theta_{n}^{J}\right|^{3}+\left(\left|\sin\omega_{n}^{J}\right|+\left|\sin\Theta_{n}^{J}\right|\right)\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sin^{2}\Psi_{n}^{j}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j,k\leq J\\
j\neq k\end{subarray}}\sin^{2}\Psi_{n}^{j}\left|\sin\Psi_{n}^{k}\right|,

Since
|\sin\omega_{n}^{J}|^{3}\lesssim|\omega_{n}^{J}|^{3}
, we have by (
7.22
)

(7.38)

\lim_{J\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left\|\frac{1}{r^{3}}\sin^{3}\omega_{n}^{J}\right\|_{L^{1}L^{2}}=0.

Furthermore,

(7.39)

\left\|1\!\!1_{\Gamma_{T}}\frac{1}{r^{3}}\sin^{3}\Theta_{n}^{J}\right\|_{L^{1}L^{2}}\lesssim\left\|1\!\!1_{\Gamma_{T}}\theta_{n}^{J}\right\|_{L^{3}L^{6}}^{3}.

We will next prove

(7.40)

\left\|\frac{1}{r^{3}}1\!\!1_{\Gamma_{T}}\sin\Theta_{n}^{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}(\sin\Psi_{n}^{j})^{2}\right\|_{L^{1}L^{2}}\lesssim\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\theta_{n}^{J}(t)\right\|_{L^{6}(|t|)}F_{n}(t)dt,

(where
F_{n}
satisfies (
7.31
)), and

(7.41)

\displaystyle\lim_{J\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left\|\frac{1}{r^{3}}1\!\!1_{\{r>|t|\}}(\sin\omega_{n}^{J})\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sin^{2}\Psi_{n}^{j}\right\|_{L^{1}L^{2}}=0

(7.42)

\displaystyle j\neq k\Longrightarrow\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|\frac{1}{r^{3}}1\!\!1_{\{r>|t|\}}\sin^{2}\Psi_{n}^{j}\sin\Psi_{n}^{k}\right\|_{L^{1}L^{2}}=0,

which will complete the proof of (
7.35
) and thus of the proposition. We first prove that there is a constant
C>0
independent of
n
such that

(7.43)

\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left\|1\!\!1_{\{r>|t|\}}\frac{1}{r}\sin(\Psi_{n}^{j})\right\|_{L^{3}L^{6}}^{2}\leq C.

Let
J_{0}\geq 1
such that for all
j\geq J_{0}
,
(\Psi_{0}^{j},\Psi_{1}^{j})\in\mathbf{H}
and
E_{M}(\Psi_{0}^{j},\Psi_{1}^{j})
is small. By the Claim
7.1
, for
j\geq J_{0}
,
E_{M}(\Psi_{0}^{j},\Psi_{1}^{j})\approx\|(\Psi_{0}^{j},\Psi_{1}^{j})\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}
. By Strichartz estimates (and the small data theory for (
4.1
) if
j\in\mathcal{J}_{C}
),
\left\|1\!\!1_{\{\{r>|t|\}}\frac{1}{r}\Psi_{n}^{j}\right\|_{L^{3}L^{6}}\lesssim\left\|(\Psi_{0}^{j},\Psi_{1}^{j})\right\|_{\mathbf{H}}
for large
j
. Since
|\sin\Psi_{n}^{j}|\leq|\Psi_{n}^{j}|
we deduce, by the Pythagorean expansion (
7.21
) of the energy,

(7.44)

\sum_{j=J_{0}}^{\infty}\left\|1\!\!1_{\{r>|t|\}}\frac{\sin(\Psi_{n}^{j})}{r}\right\|_{L^{3}L^{6}}^{2}\leq C.

Let
j\in\{1,\ldots,J_{0}-1\}
. If
j\in\mathcal{J}_{C}
,

(7.45)

\left\|1\!\!1_{\{r>|t|\}}\frac{\sin(\Psi_{n}^{j})}{r}\right\|_{L^{3}L^{6}}=\left\|1\!\!1_{\{r>|t|\}}\frac{\sin(\Psi^{j})}{r}\right\|_{L^{3}L^{6}},

which is finite by Lemma
7.5
, and if
j\in\mathcal{J}_{L}
,

(7.46)

\left\|1\!\!1_{\{r>|t|\}}\frac{\sin\Psi_{n}^{j}}{r}\right\|_{L^{3}L^{6}}\leq\left\|1\!\!1_{\{r>|t|\}}\frac{\Psi_{L}^{j}}{r}\right\|_{L^{3}L^{6}},

which is finite by Strichartz estimates. Thus (
7.43
) holds.

Proof of (
7.40
)
. We have

(7.47)

\left\|\frac{1}{r^{3}}1\!\!1_{\Gamma_{T}}\sin\Theta_{n}^{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sin^{2}\Psi_{n}^{j}\right\|_{L^{1}L^{2}}=\int_{0}^{T}\bigg\|\frac{1}{r^{3}}\sin\Theta_{n}^{J}(t)\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sin^{2}\Psi_{n}^{j}(t)\bigg\|_{L^{2}(|t|)}dt\\
\leq\int_{0}^{T}\Big\|\frac{1}{r}\Theta_{n}^{J}(t)\Big\|_{L^{6}(|t|)}\bigg\|\frac{1}{r^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sin^{2}\Psi_{n}^{j}(t)\bigg\|_{L^{3}(|t|)}dt.

Furthermore,

(7.48)

\bigg\|\frac{1}{r^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sin^{2}\Psi_{n}^{j}(t)\bigg\|_{L^{3}(|t|)}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{J}\left\|\frac{\sin^{2}\Psi_{n}^{j}(t)}{r^{2}}\right\|_{L^{3}(|t|)}=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\left\|\frac{\sin\Psi_{n}^{j}(t)}{r}\right\|^{2}_{L^{6}(|t|)}.

Let

F_{n}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left\|\frac{\sin\Psi_{n}^{j}(t)}{r}\right\|^{2}_{L^{6}(|t|)}.

Then

(7.49)

\|F_{n}\|_{L^{3/2}}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left\|1\!\!1_{\{r>|t|\}}\frac{\sin\Psi_{n}^{J}}{r}\right\|^{2}_{L^{3}L^{6}}\leq C

by (
7.44
). Combining (
7.47
), (
7.48
) and (
7.49
) we obtain (
7.40
).

Proof of (
7.41
)
. We have

\left\|\frac{1\!\!1_{\{r>|t|\}}}{r^{3}}(\sin\omega_{n}^{J})\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sin^{2}\Psi_{n}^{j}\right\|_{L^{1}L^{2}}\\
\leq\left\|\frac{1\!\!1_{\{r>|t|\}}}{r}\sin\omega_{n}^{J}\right\|_{L^{3}L^{6}}\left\|\frac{1\!\!1_{\{r>|t|\}}}{r^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\sin^{2}\Psi_{n}^{j}\right\|_{L^{3/2}L^{3}}\\
\leq\left\|\frac{1\!\!1_{\{r>|t|\}}}{r}\sin\omega_{n}^{J}\right\|_{L^{3}L^{6}}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left\|1\!\!1_{\{r>|t|\}}\frac{\sin\Psi_{n}^{J}}{r}\right\|^{2}_{L^{3}L^{6}},

and (
7.41
) follows from
(
7.22
) and (
7.43
).

Proof of (
7.42
)
. If
j\in\mathcal{J}_{L}
, we have by dominated convergence, as in (
7.37
),

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|1\!\!1_{\{r>|t|\}}\frac{1}{r}\sin\Psi_{n}^{j}\right\|_{L^{3}L^{6}}=0.

Thus (
7.42
) holds if
j\in\mathcal{J}_{L}
or
k\in\mathcal{J}_{L}
. If
j\in\mathcal{J}_{C}
and
k\in\mathcal{J}_{C}
, (
7.42
) follows from

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\lambda_{j,n}}{\lambda_{k,n}}+\frac{\lambda_{k,n}}{\lambda_{j,n}}=\infty,

and Lemma
7.5
.
∎

7.2.
Proof of the soliton resolution

In this subsection, we deduce Theorem
3
from the rigidity theorem (Theorem
4.2
). The proof is close to the proof of Theorem
4
and we will not give all the details. As in the proof of Theorem
4
, we will focus on the finite time blow-up case, the proof for global solutions being very similar.

7.2.1.
Analysis along a sequence of times

We let
\psi
be a solution of (
1.1
) such that
T_{+}=T_{+}(\psi)<\infty
, and
(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1})
as in Proposition
7.3
. The core of the proof of Theorem
3
is the following proposition, which is the analog of Proposition
6.4
. Recall from (
2.1
) the definition of the norm
\|\cdot\|_{H}
.

Proposition 7.9
.

Let
\{t_{n}\}_{n}
be a sequence of times such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}t_{n}=T_{+},\quad\forall n,\;0\leq t_{n}<T_{+}.

Then there exists a subsequence of
\{t_{n}\}_{n}
(that we still denote by
\{t_{n}\}_{n}
),
J\geq 1
,
(\iota_{j})_{j}\in\{\pm 1\}^{J}
, sequences
\{\lambda_{j,n}\}_{n}
,
1\leq j\leq J
with

\lambda_{1,n}\ll\ldots\ll\lambda_{J,n}\ll T_{+}-t_{n},

such that

(7.50)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|\psi(t_{n})-\varphi_{0}-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\frac{\iota_{j}}{\lambda_{j,n}}Q\left(\frac{\cdot}{\lambda_{j,n}}\right)\right\|_{H}=0

(7.51)

\displaystyle E_{M}(\psi_{0},\psi_{1})=E_{M}(\psi_{0},\psi_{1})+JE_{M}(Q,0)+\frac{1}{2}\left\|\partial_{t}\psi(t_{n})-\psi_{1}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+o_{n}(1).

Proof.

The proof of Proposition
7.9
is close to the proof of Proposition
6.4
, but somehow easier, since solutions of the co-rotational maps equation (
1.1
) with initial data at
t=0
are always defined for
\{r>|t|\}
and enjoy a global space-time bound there (see Lemma
7.5
).

To simplify notations, we assume without loss of generality in all the proof that

T_{+}(\psi)=1.

Step 1. Profile decomposition.
We let
\varphi
be the solution of (
1.1
) with initial data
(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1})
at
t=1
. By Proposition
7.3
,

\supp(\varphi-\psi)\subset\{r\leq 1-t\}.

Extracting subsequences, we assume that
\Big\{\vec{\psi}(t_{n})-(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1})\Big\}_{n}
has a profile decomposition as in §
7.1.4
. We will use the notations of §
7.1.4
:
\Psi_{n}^{j}
,
\{\lambda_{j,n}\}_{n}
,
\{t_{j,n}\}_{n}
etc… for this decomposition. Recall the partition
\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}=\mathcal{J}_{C}\cup\mathcal{J}_{L}
of the set of indices defined after Proposition
7.7
. Rescaling the profiles, we can assume that there is another partition
\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}=\mathcal{I}_{s}\cup\mathcal{I}_{c}\cup\mathcal{I}_{+}\cup\mathcal{I}_{-}
defined by

\displaystyle j\in\mathcal{I}_{s}

\displaystyle\iff j\in\mathcal{J}_{C},\;\Psi_{0}^{j}\in\{Q,-Q\}\cup\{m\pi,\;m\in\mathbb{Z}\}\text{ and }\Psi_{1}^{j}=0,

\displaystyle j\in\mathcal{I}_{c}

\displaystyle\iff j\in\mathcal{J}_{C}\text{ and }\Psi^{j}\text{ is not a stationary solution of \eqref{NLW}},

\displaystyle j\in\mathcal{I}_{\pm}

\displaystyle\iff j\in\mathcal{J}_{L}\text{ and }\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{-t_{j,n}}{\lambda_{j,n}}=\pm\infty.

In Step 2, we will prove that
\mathcal{I}_{c}=\emptyset
, in Step 3, that
\mathcal{I}_{\pm}=\emptyset
. In Step 4, we exclude self-similar behaviour for the scaling parameters. In Step 5, we conclude the proof, showing that the first component of the dispersive remainder
\omega_{0,n}^{J}
goes to
0
in
H
as
n
goes to infinity.

Step 2. Compact profiles.
We prove that
\mathcal{I}_{c}
is empty by contradiction. Assume that it is not empty and let
j_{0}\in\mathcal{J}_{c}
. Assume
(\Psi_{0}^{j_{0}},\Psi_{1}^{j_{0}})\in\mathbf{H}_{\ell,m}
.

By Theorem
1
, there exists
\eta_{1}>0
such that the following holds for all
t\geq 0
or for all
t\leq 0
:

(7.52)

\int_{|t|}^{\infty}\left((\partial_{t,r}\Psi^{j_{0}}(t,r))^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\sin^{2}\Psi^{j_{0}}(t,r)\right)rdr\geq\eta_{1}.

The sequence
\big\{\vec{\psi}(t_{n})\big\}_{n}
has the same profile decomposition as the sequence
\big\{\vec{\psi}(t_{n})-(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1})\big\}_{n}
, with an additional profile

(\Psi_{0}^{0},\Psi_{1}^{0})=(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1}),\quad\forall n,\;\lambda_{j_{0},n}=1,\;t_{j_{0},n}=0.

By Proposition
7.8
and arguing as in Step 3 of the proof of Proposition
6.4
, we obtain, for all
r>|\tau|
,

(7.53)

\psi(t_{n}+\tau)=\varphi(t_{n}+\tau)+\sum_{j=1}^{J}\Psi_{n}^{j}(\tau,r)+\omega_{n}^{J}(\tau,r)+\Theta_{n}^{J}(\tau,r),

where

(7.54)

\lim_{J\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\tau}\left\|\vec{\Theta}_{n}^{J}(\tau)\right\|_{\mathbf{H}(\tau)}=0.

We distinguish between two cases.

Case 1. Channels of energy in the future.
Assume that (
7.52
) holds for all
t>0
. Then by (
7.53
) at
\tau=1-t_{n}
,

\sum_{j=1}^{J}\Psi_{n}^{j}(1-t_{n},r)+\omega_{n}^{J}(1-t_{n},r)+\Theta_{n}^{J}(1-t_{n},r)=0,\quad r>1-t_{n},

which yields

(7.55)

\int_{1-t_{n}}^{\infty}\Big(\partial_{t,r}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{J}\Psi_{n}^{j}(1-t_{n},r)+\omega_{n}^{J}(1-t_{n},r)+\Theta_{n}^{J}(1-t_{n},r)\Big)\Big)^{2}rdr\\
+\int_{1-t_{n}}^{\infty}\sin^{2}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{J}\Psi_{n}^{j}(1-t_{n},r)+\omega_{n}^{J}(1-t_{n},r)+\Theta_{n}^{J}(1-t_{n},r)\Big)\frac{dr}{r}=0.

Fixing
J
large, and using that (
7.52
) holds for
t\geq 0
, we claim that the left-hand side of (
7.55
) is larger than

\frac{1}{2}\int_{1-t_{n}}^{\infty}\left(\partial_{t,r}\Psi_{n}^{j_{0}}(1-t_{n},r)\right)^{2}rdr+\frac{1}{2}\int_{1-t_{n}}^{\infty}\frac{\sin^{2}\Psi_{n}^{j_{0}}(1-t_{n},r)}{r}dr\geq\frac{\eta_{1}}{2}

for large
n
, a contradiction. In view of (
7.54
), and Claim
7.2
, it is sufficient to prove

(7.56)

\displaystyle j\neq j_{0}\Longrightarrow\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{1-t_{n}}^{\infty}\partial_{t,r}\Psi_{n}^{j}(1-t_{n},r)\cdot\partial_{t,r}\Psi_{n}^{j_{0}}(1-t_{n},r)rdr=0

(7.57)

\displaystyle j\neq j_{0}\Longrightarrow\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{1-t_{n}}^{\infty}\left|\sin\Psi_{n}^{j}(1-t_{n},r)\sin\Psi_{n}^{j_{0}}(1-t_{n},r)\right|\frac{dr}{r}=0

(7.58)

\displaystyle 1\leq j\leq J\Longrightarrow\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{1-t_{n}}^{\infty}\partial_{t,r}\Psi_{n}^{j}(1-t_{n},r)\cdot\partial_{t,r}\omega_{n}^{J}(1-t_{n},r)rdr=0

(7.59)

\displaystyle 1\leq j\leq J\Longrightarrow\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{1-t_{n}}^{\infty}\left|\sin\Psi_{n}^{j}(1-t_{n},r)\sin\omega_{n}^{J}(1-t_{n},r)\right|\frac{dr}{r}=0.

The proof of (
7.56
),…,(
7.58
) is very close to the proofs of the corresponding properties for the linear wave profiles in Appendix
B.1
and of the Pythagorean expansion (
7.21
) of the wave maps energy in Appendix
B.2
and we omit it.

Case 2. Channels of energy in the past.

We assume that (
7.52
) holds for all
t\leq 0
. By (
7.53
) at
\tau=-t_{n}
,

(7.60)

\int_{t_{n}}^{\infty}\Big|\partial_{t,r}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{J}\Psi_{n}^{j}(-t_{n},r)+\omega_{n}^{J}(-t_{n},r)+\Theta_{n}^{J}(-t_{n},r)\Big)\Big|^{2}rdr\\
+\int_{t_{n}}^{\infty}\sin^{2}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{J}\Psi_{n}^{j}(-t_{n},r)+\omega_{n}^{J}(1-t_{n},r)+\Theta_{n}^{J}(-t_{n},r)\Big)\frac{dr}{r}\\
=\int_{t_{n}}^{\infty}\left|\partial_{t,r}\psi(0,r)-\partial_{t,r}\varphi(0,r)\right|^{2}rdr+\int_{t_{n}}^{\infty}\sin^{2}(\psi(0,r)-\varphi(0,r))\frac{dr}{r}\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}0.

By the same argument as in Case 1, we obtain that the left-hand side of (
7.60
) is larger than

\frac{1}{2}\int_{t_{n}}^{\infty}\left(\partial_{t,r}\Psi_{n}^{j_{0}}(-t_{n},r)\right)^{2}rdr+\frac{1}{2}\int_{t_{n}}^{\infty}\frac{\sin^{2}\Psi_{n}^{j_{0}}(-t_{n},r)}{r}dr\geq\frac{\eta_{1}}{2},

a contradiction.

Step 3. Scattering profiles.
In this step we prove by contradiction that
\mathcal{I}_{+}
is empty. This step is very close to Case 1 of the preceding step, and also to Step 4 in the proof of Proposition
6.4
, and we only sketch it. The proof that
\mathcal{I}_{-}
is empty is very similar and we omit it.

Assume that
\mathcal{I}_{+}\neq\emptyset
and let
j_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{+}
. Since
j_{0}\in\mathcal{J}_{L}
, we have

\Psi_{n}^{j_{0}}(t,r)=\Psi^{j_{0}}\left(\frac{t-t_{j_{0},n}}{\lambda_{j_{0},n}},\frac{r}{\lambda_{j_{0},n}}\right)

where
\Psi^{j_{0}}
is a solution of the linearized equation (
7.6
), with nonzero initial data in
\mathbf{H}
. Then
U^{j_{0}}=\frac{1}{r}\Psi^{j_{0}}
is a radial solution of the linear wave equation in space dimension
4
, and we deduce as in Step 4 of the proof of Proposition
6.4
that there exists
\eta_{1}>0
, such that
for large
n
,

\int_{1-t_{n}}^{\infty}\left|\partial_{t,r}U_{n}^{j_{0}}(1-t_{n},r)\right|^{2}r^{3}dr\geq 2\eta_{1},

where
U_{n}^{j_{0}}(t,r)=\frac{1}{\lambda_{j_{0},n}}U^{j_{0}}\left(\frac{t-t_{j_{0},n}}{\lambda_{j_{0},n}},\frac{r}{\lambda_{j_{0},n}}\right)
. As a consequence, for large
n

\int_{1-t_{n}}^{\infty}\left|\partial_{t,r}\Psi_{n}^{j_{0}}(1-t_{n},r)\right|^{2}rdr+\int_{1-t_{n}}^{\infty}\left|\Psi_{n}^{j_{0}}(1-t_{n},r)\right|^{2}\frac{dr}{r}\geq 2\eta_{1}.

Using that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\Psi_{n}^{j_{0}}(1-t_{n})\|_{L^{\infty}}=0
(see (
2.5
)), we deduce that for large
n
,

\int_{1-t_{n}}^{\infty}\left|\partial_{t,r}\Psi_{n}^{j_{0}}(1-t_{n},r)\right|^{2}rdr+\int_{1-t_{n}}^{\infty}\sin^{2}\Psi_{n}^{j_{0}}(1-t_{n},r)\frac{dr}{r}\geq\eta_{1}.

The end of the proof is the same as in Case 1 of Step 2 above, and we omit.

Step 4. Bound on the scaling parameters.
According to the preceding steps,
(\omega_{0,n}^{J},\omega_{1,n}^{J})
is, for large
J
, independent of
J
, and we will drop the superscript
J
. Furthermore, all the profiles are in
\mathcal{I}_{s}
. Using the Pythagorean expansion of the energy (
7.21
) for the sequence
\vec{\psi}(t_{n})
, we see that there exists
J_{0}\geq 0
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
,
(\iota_{j})_{1\leq j\leq J_{0}}\in\{\pm 1\}^{J_{0}}
, sequences
\{\lambda_{j,n}\}_{n}
with
0<\lambda_{1,n}\ll\ldots\ll\lambda_{J_{0},n}
such that

(7.61)

\vec{\psi}(t_{n})=\vec{\varphi}(t_{n})+(m\pi,0)+\sum_{j=1}^{J_{0}}\left(\iota_{j}Q\left(\frac{\cdot}{\lambda_{j,n}}\right),0\right)+\left(\omega_{0,n},\omega_{1,n}\right)+o_{n}(1)\text{ in }\mathbf{H}.

Using that
(\omega_{0,n},\omega_{1,n})\in\mathbf{H}
, and that
\psi(t_{n},r)=\varphi(t_{n},r)
for
r>1-t_{n}
, we see that
m=-\sum_{j=1}^{J_{0}}\iota_{j}
. In this step, we prove

(7.62)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\lambda_{J_{0},n}}{1-t_{n}}=0.

Arguing by contradiction and extracting subsequences, we assume that there exists
R>0
such that for large
n
,

\lambda_{j_{0},n}\geq R(1-t_{n}).

By (
7.61
), and the fact that
\psi(t_{n},r)-\varphi(t_{n},r)=0
for
r>1-t_{n}
, we obtain that

(7.63)

\omega_{0,n}(\lambda_{J_{0},n}r)+m\pi+\sum_{j=1}^{J_{0}-1}\iota_{j}Q\left(\frac{\lambda_{J_{0},n}r}{\lambda_{j,n}}\right)+Q(r)=o_{n}(1)\text{ in }H(R).

Consider
\chi\in C_{0}^{\infty}\big((R,\infty)\big)
, nonnegative and not identically zero. Since
\omega_{0,n}(\lambda_{J_{0},n}\cdot)
converges to
0
weakly in
H
, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\omega_{0,n}(\lambda_{J_{0},n}r)\chi(r)dr=0.

Furthermore, for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,J_{0}-1\}
, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\iota_{j}\int_{0}^{\infty}Q\left(\frac{\lambda_{J_{0},n}r}{\lambda_{j,n}}\right)\chi(r)dr=\iota_{j}\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi(r)dr.

Thus (
7.63
) implies, letting
n\to\infty
,

\bigg(m+\sum_{j=1}^{J_{0}-1}\iota_{j}\bigg)\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi(r)dr+\iota_{J_{0}}\int_{0}^{\infty}Q(r)\chi(r)dr=0.

Using that
m+\sum_{j=1}^{J_{0}-1}\iota_{j}=-\iota_{J_{0}}
, we deduce

\iota_{J_{0}}\int_{0}^{\infty}(Q-\pi)(r)\chi(r)dr=0,

a contradiction since
Q<\pi
.

Step 5. Dispersive remainder.

In this step we prove

(7.64)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\omega_{0,n}\|_{H}=0

which will conclude the proof of Proposition
7.9
. Using that
(\omega_{0,n},\omega_{1,n})\in\mathbf{H}
, we obtain as usual that
w_{n}=\frac{1}{r}\omega_{n}
is a radial finite energy solution of the free wave equation (
3.1
) in space dimension
4
. Thus by the result of
[
12
]
(see (
3.4
)),

\sum_{\pm}\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\int_{|t|}^{\infty}\left|\partial_{t,r}w_{n}(t,r)\right|^{2}r^{3}dr\gtrsim\|w_{0,n}\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{1}},

where
w_{0,n}=\frac{1}{r}\omega_{0,n}
.
Using the decay of the exterior energy for the linear wave equation, we obtain that the following holds for all
t\geq 0
or for all
t\leq 0

\int_{|t|}^{\infty}\left|\partial_{t,r}w_{n}(t,r)\right|^{2}r^{3}dr\gtrsim\|w_{0,n}\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{1}}.

Going back to
\omega_{n}
, we deduce that the following holds for all
t\geq 0
or for all
t\leq 0
.

\int_{|t|}^{\infty}\left|\partial_{t,r}\omega_{n}(t,r)\right|^{2}rdr+\int_{|t|}^{\infty}\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left(\omega_{n}(t,r)\right)^{2}rdr\gtrsim\|\omega_{0,n}\|_{H}^{2}.

Since by (
7.22
),
\lim_{n}\|w_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}=0
, we deduce that for all
t\geq 0
or for all
t\leq 0

\int_{|t|}^{\infty}\left|\partial_{t,r}\omega_{n}(t,r)\right|^{2}rdr+\int_{|t|}^{\infty}\sin^{2}\omega_{n}(t,r)\frac{dr}{r}\gtrsim\|\omega_{0,n}\|_{H}^{2}.

The end of the proof of (
7.64
) is similar to Step 2, and we omit it.
∎

7.2.2.
End of the proof of the soliton resolution

The end of the proof of Theorem
3
in the finite time blow-up case is close to the end of the proof of Theorem
4
. However, the fact that the energy always gives an a priori bound on a solution
\psi
of the wave maps equation (
1.1
) makes the proof slightly simpler.

As before, we let
\psi
be a solution of (
1.1
) such that
T_{+}(\psi)=1
. By Proposition
7.3
, there exists a sequence of times
\{t_{n}\}\to 1
such that

(7.65)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\partial_{t}\psi(t_{n})-\varphi_{1}\|_{L^{2}}=0.

(Here and until the end of this proof,
L^{2}
denotes the space
L^{2}\big((0,\infty)\big)
with respect to the measure
rdr
.)
Combining (
7.65
) with Proposition
7.9
, we obtain (extracting subsequences) that there exists
J\geq 0
,
(\iota_{j})_{j}\in\{\pm 1\}^{J}
, scaling parameters
0<\lambda_{1,n}\ll\ldots\ll\lambda_{J,n}
such that

(7.66)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\bigg\|\vec{\psi}(t_{n})-(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1})-(m\pi,0)-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\left(\iota_{j}Q(\lambda_{j,n}\cdot),0\right)\bigg\|_{\mathbf{H}}=0.

Since
t_{n}\to 1
and
1
is the maximal time of existence of
\psi
, we must have
J\geq 1
. Using the Pythagorean expansion (
7.21
) of the energy, we see that

(7.67)

E_{M}(\psi_{0},\psi_{1})=E_{M}(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1})+JE_{M}(Q,0).

We next prove by contradiction

(7.68)

\lim_{t\to 1}\|\partial_{t}\psi(t)-\varphi_{1}\|_{L^{2}}=0.

If (
7.68
) does not hold, using (
7.65
) and the intermediate value theorem, there exists a sequence of times
t_{n}^{\prime}\to 1
such that

(7.69)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|\partial_{t}\psi(t_{n}^{\prime})-\varphi_{1}\right\|_{L^{2}}=\varepsilon_{0}.

Using Proposition
7.9
along the sequence
\{t_{n}^{\prime}\}_{n}
, we obtain (extracting subsequences)
J^{\prime}\geq 0
,
(\iota_{j}^{\prime})_{j}\in\{\pm 1\}^{J^{\prime}}
,
0<\lambda_{1,n}^{\prime}\ll\ldots\ll\lambda_{J^{\prime},n}^{\prime}
such that

(7.70)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\bigg\|\psi(t_{n}^{\prime})-\varphi_{0}-m\pi-\sum_{j=1}^{J^{\prime}}\iota_{j}^{\prime}Q\bigg(\frac{\cdot}{\lambda_{j,n}^{\prime}}\bigg)\bigg\|_{H}=0.

This yields

(7.71)

E_{M}(\psi_{0},\psi_{1})=E_{M}(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1})+\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{0}^{2}+J^{\prime}E_{M}(Q,0).

Combining with (
7.67
), we see that
\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{0}^{2}=(J-J^{\prime})E_{M}(Q,0)
, which yields a contradiction since
\varepsilon_{0}
is small and not equal to
0
, concluding the proof of (
7.67
).

Combining Proposition
7.9
, the conservation of the energy and (
7.68
), we obtain that
J=\frac{E_{M}(\psi_{0},\psi_{1})-E_{M}(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1})}{E_{M}(Q,0)}
is a positive integer, and that
for all sequences of times
t_{n}\to 1
, there exists a subsequence of
\{t_{n}\}_{n}
,
(\iota_{j})_{j}\in\{\pm 1\}^{J}
, and sequences
\{\lambda_{j,n}\}_{n}
,
1\leq j\leq J
with

0<\lambda_{1,n}\ll\ldots\ll\lambda_{J,n}\ll 1-t_{n},

such that

(7.72)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\bigg\|\vec{\psi}(t_{n})-(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1})-m\pi-\sum_{j=1}^{J}\bigg(\iota_{j}Q\bigg(\frac{\cdot}{\lambda_{j,n}}\bigg),0\bigg)\bigg\|_{\mathbf{H}}=0,

where
m=\sum\iota_{j}
.

The fact that we can choose the
\iota_{j}
(and thus
m
) independently of the sequence
\{t_{n}^{\prime}\}_{n}
, and that the preceding statement implies the conclusion of Theorem
3
(with
\phi_{0}=\varphi_{0}-m\pi
,
\phi_{1}=\varphi_{1}
) is standard and we omit it (see the end of Section
6
above, and Section 3.5 of
[
20
]
).

The proof of Theorem
3
in the case where
T_{+}(\psi)=+\infty
is very close, using the scattering state
\psi_{L}
(defined in Proposition
7.4
) instead of
(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1})
. We again omit the details.

Appendix A
Radiation term for the free wave equation

In this appendix we consider the linear inhomogeneous wave equation in any space dimension
N\geq 3
:

(A.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}^{2}u-\Delta u&=f,\quad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}\\
\vec{u}_{\restriction t=0}&=(u_{0},u_{1}),\end{aligned}\right.

with radial data.

Proposition A.1
.

Let

(u_{0},u_{1})\in\mathcal{H}=(\dot{H}^{1}\times L^{2})_{\rad}(\mathbb{R}^{N}),\quad f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R},L^{2}_{\rad}(\mathbb{R}^{N})),

and
u
be the corresponding solution of (
A.1
). Then there exists
G\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
such that

(A.2)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\left|r^{\frac{N-1}{2}}\partial_{r}u(t,r)-G(r-t)\right|^{2}dr

\displaystyle=0

(A.3)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\left|r^{\frac{N-1}{2}}\partial_{t}u(t,r)+G(r-t)\right|^{2}dr

\displaystyle=0

If furthermore
(u_{0},u_{1})\in\dot{H}^{2}\times\dot{H}^{1}
and
\partial_{t}f\in L^{1}L^{2}
, then

(A.4)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\left|r^{\frac{N-1}{2}}\partial_{r}\partial_{t}u(t,r)+G^{\prime}(r-t)\right|^{2}dr

\displaystyle=0

(A.5)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\left|r^{\frac{N-1}{2}}\partial_{t}^{2}u(t,r)-G^{\prime}(r-t)\right|^{2}dr

\displaystyle=0

Proof.

The fact that (
A.2
) and (
A.3
) hold in the case
f=0
is well-known (see
[
24
, Theorem 2.1]
) and goes back at least to the work of Friedlander
[
33
]
.

To prove (
A.2
) and (
A.3
) in the general case, one can reduce to the case
f=0
, by recalling that if
u
is a solution of (
A.1
) with
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R},L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N}))
, there exists a finite energy solution
v_{L}
of the free wave equation
(\partial_{t}^{2}-\Delta)v_{L}=0
such that

(A.6)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\left\|\vec{u}(t)-\vec{v}_{L}(t)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}=0.

This follows immediately from the Duhamel formulation of (
A.1
), which yields that (
A.6
) holds with

v_{L}(t)=\cos(t\sqrt{-\Delta})\left(u_{0}-\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{\sin(s\sqrt{-\Delta})}{\sqrt{-\Delta}}f(s)ds\right)\\
+\frac{\sin(t\sqrt{-\Delta})}{\sqrt{-\Delta}}\left(u_{1}+\int_{0}^{+\infty}\cos\left(s\sqrt{-\Delta}\right)f(s)ds\right).

We next assume that
u_{0}\in\dot{H}^{2}
,
u_{1}\in\dot{H}^{1}
,
\partial_{t}f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R},L^{2})
and prove (
A.4
), (
A.5
). We first note that
\partial_{t}u
is solution of

(A.7)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}^{2}\partial_{t}u-\Delta\partial_{t}u&=\partial_{t}f,\quad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}\\
\vec{u}_{\restriction t=0}&=(u_{1},\Delta u_{0}+f(0))\in\mathcal{H},\end{aligned}\right.

(where
f(0)
is well defined and in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
by an elementary trace lemma). By the considerations above, there exists
H\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
such that

(A.8)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\left|r^{\frac{N-1}{2}}\partial_{r}\partial_{t}u(t,r)-H(r-t)\right|^{2}dr

\displaystyle=0

(A.9)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\left|r^{\frac{N-1}{2}}\partial_{t}^{2}u(t,r)+H(r-t)\right|^{2}dr

\displaystyle=0,

and we are reduced to prove that
H=-G^{\prime}
.

Let
\chi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\chi(\eta)=0
if
\eta\leq\frac{1}{2}
and
\chi(\eta)=1
if
\eta\geq 1
. Since

\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{0}^{1}|G(r-t)|^{2}dr=0,

the equality (
A.3
) implies

\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|\chi(r)r^{\frac{N-1}{2}}\partial_{t}u(t,r)+G(r-t)\right|^{2}dr=0,

that is

\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|(\eta+\tau)^{\frac{N-1}{2}}\chi(\eta+\tau)\partial_{t}u(\tau,\eta+\tau)+G(\eta)\right|^{2}d\eta=0.

In other words, the family of functions

\eta\mapsto(\eta+\tau)^{\frac{N-1}{2}}\chi(\eta+\tau)(\partial_{t}u)(\tau,\eta+\tau)

converge to
-G
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
as
\tau
goes to infinity. Taking the derivative in
\eta
and using that

\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}|\partial_{t}u(t,r)|^{2}r^{N-3}dr=0

(which follows from the fact that there exists a finite energy solution
w_{L}
of
\partial_{t}^{2}w_{L}-\Delta w_{L}=0

such that

\lim_{t\to\infty}\|\partial_{t}u(t)-w_{L}(t)\|_{\dot{H}^{1}}=0,
and a classical dispersive estimate, see e.g.
[
24
, Theorem 2.1]
)
we obtain that the family of functions

\eta\mapsto(\eta+\tau)^{\frac{N-1}{2}}\chi(\eta+\tau)(\partial_{t}\partial_{r}u)(\tau,\eta+\tau)

converge to
-G^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
as
\tau\to\infty
. However by (
A.8
), we obtain that this family converge to
H
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
as
\tau\to\infty
. By uniqueness of the distributional limit,
H=-G^{\prime}
concluding the proof.
∎

Remark A.2
.

We recall that for all
G\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, there exists a unique finite energy solution
u
of (
A.1
) such that (
A.2
) and (
A.3
) hold: see again
[
24
, Theorem 2.1]
.

Appendix B
Pseudo-ortogonality and channels of energy

B.1.
Linear wave equation

In this subsection, we prove (
6.10
) and (
6.11
)

Since
\int_{\rho_{n}<|x|<\rho_{n}^{\prime}}\ldots=\int_{\rho_{n}<|x|}\ldots-\int_{\rho_{n}^{\prime}<|x|}
, it is sufficient to treat the case where
\rho_{n}^{\prime}=\infty
for all
n
. Rescaling
u_{n}
, we also assume

\forall n,\quad\lambda_{j,n}=1.

Pseudo-orthogonality between two profiles.

We first prove (
6.10
).
We will use the pseudo-orthogonality (
6.1
) of the scaling and time translation parameters.
We fix
j\neq k
. Let

\varepsilon_{n}:=\int_{|x|>\rho_{n}}\nabla_{t,x}U^{j}_{n}(s_{n},x)\cdot\nabla_{t,x}U^{k}_{n}(s_{n},x)dx\\
=\int_{|x|>\rho_{n}}\nabla_{t,x}U^{j}\left(s_{n}-t_{j,n},x\right)\cdot\frac{1}{\lambda_{k,n}^{2}}\nabla_{t,x}U^{k}\left(\frac{s_{n}-t_{k,n}}{\lambda_{k,n}},\frac{x}{\lambda_{k,n}}\right)dx.

Arguing by contradiction, we can extract subsequences and assume that the following limits exist in
\mathbb{R}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
.

\lim_{n\to\infty}s_{n}-t_{j,n}=T_{j},\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{s_{n}-t_{k,n}}{\lambda_{k,n}}=T_{k}.

Case 1:
T_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
,
T_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
.
In this case
we have

\varepsilon_{n}=o_{n}(1)+\int_{|x|>\rho_{n}}\nabla_{t,x}U^{j}(T_{j},x)\cdot\frac{1}{\lambda_{k,n}^{2}}\nabla_{t,x}U^{k}\left(T_{k},\frac{x}{\lambda_{k,n}}\right)dx.

Since (
6.1
) implies
\lim_{n}\lambda_{k,n}\in\{0,\infty\}
, we obtain right away
\lim_{n}\varepsilon_{n}=0
.

Case 2.
T_{j}=+\infty
,
T_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
.
We note that in this case, there exists
g^{j}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
such that

(B.1)

\varepsilon_{n}=o_{n}(1)+\int_{\rho_{n}}^{\infty}g^{j}(r-s_{n}+t_{j,n})\frac{1}{\lambda_{k,n}^{2}}\partial_{t}U^{k}\left(T_{k},\frac{r}{\lambda_{k,n}}\right)r^{3/2}dr\\
-\int_{\rho_{n}}^{\infty}g^{j}(r-s_{n}+t_{j,n})\frac{1}{\lambda_{k,n}^{2}}\partial_{r}U^{k}\left(T_{k},\frac{r}{\lambda_{k,n}}\right)r^{3/2}dr.

Indeed if
\lim_{n\to\infty}t_{j,n}\in\{\pm\infty\}
,
U^{j}
is a solution of the free wave equation (
3.1
), and (
B.1
) follows from the asymptotic behaviour for these solutions, recalled in Proposition
A.1
above. If
t_{j,n}=0
for all
n
, then by the definition of
R_{n}
and the assumptions
\rho_{n}\geq R_{n}+|s_{n}|
, there exists a solution
V^{j}_{L}
of (
3.1
) such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{|x|>\rho_{n}}\left|\nabla_{t,x}U^{j}(s_{n}-t_{j,n},x)-\nabla_{t,x}V^{j}_{L}(s_{n}-t_{j,n},x)\right|^{2}dx=0,

and the claim follows again from Proposition
A.1
. Letting
h^{k}=r^{3/2}\partial_{t}U^{k}(T_{k},r)\in L^{2}(0,\infty)
, we see that

\int_{\rho_{n}}^{\infty}g^{j}(r-s_{n}+t_{j,n})\frac{1}{\lambda_{k,n}^{2}}\partial_{t}U^{k}\left(T_{k},\frac{r}{\lambda_{k,n}}\right)r^{3/2}dr=\int_{\rho_{n}}^{\infty}g^{j}(r-s_{n}+t_{j,n})h^{k}\left(\frac{r}{\lambda_{k,n}}\right)dr,

which tends to
0
as
n
tends to infinity because
\lim_{n}s_{n}-t_{j,n}=\infty
. Since the term in the second line of (
B.1
) can be treated in the exact same way, we obtain
\lim_{n\to\infty}\varepsilon_{n}=0
.

The proof in the cases
T_{j}=-\infty
,
T_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
, and
T_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
,
T_{k}=\pm\infty
are very close and we omit them.

Case 3.
T_{j}=+\infty
and
T_{k}=+\infty
.
Similarly to Case
2
, there exist
g^{j}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
g^{k}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
such that

\varepsilon_{n}=o_{n}(1)+2\int_{\rho_{n}}^{\infty}g^{j}(r-s_{n}+t_{j,n})\frac{1}{\lambda_{k,n}^{2}}g^{k}\left(\frac{r-s_{n}+t_{k,n}}{\lambda_{k,n}}\right)dr,

and the fact that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\varepsilon_{n}=0
follows easily from the pseudo-orthogonality property (
6.1
).

The proof in the case
T_{j}=-\infty
,
T_{k}=-\infty
is the same and we omit it.

Case 4.
T_{j}=+\infty
and
T_{k}=-\infty
.

Using Proposition
A.1
as in cases
2
and
3
, we obtain that the contributions of the integrals of
\partial_{t}U^{j}_{n}\partial_{t}U^{k}_{n}
and
\partial_{r}U^{j}_{n}\partial_{r}U^{k}_{n}
in the definition of
\varepsilon_{n}
are opposite, and thus

\lim_{n\to\infty}\varepsilon_{n}=0.

The proof in the case
T_{j}=-\infty
,
T_{k}=+\infty
is exactly the same.

Pseudo-orthogonality between a profile and the dispersive remainder.

We next prove (
6.11
).
We assume again that
T_{j}=\lim_{n\to\infty}s_{n}-t_{j,n}
exists in
\mathbb{R}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
.

Case 1.
T_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
.

Then

\varepsilon_{n}=o_{n}(1)+\int_{|x|>\rho_{n}}\nabla_{t,x}U^{j}(T_{j},x)\nabla_{t,x}w^{J}_{n}(s_{n},x)dx.

If
\lim_{n\to\infty}\rho_{n}=+\infty
, we deduce immediately that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\varepsilon_{n}=0
. If not, we can assume
\lim_{n\to\infty}\rho_{n}=\rho_{\infty}\in[0,\infty)
, and we obtain

\varepsilon_{n}=o_{n}(1)+\int_{|x|>\rho_{\infty}}\nabla_{t,x}U^{j}(T_{j},x)\nabla_{t,x}w^{J}_{n}(s_{n},x)dx.

By (
6.10
), we can take
J
arbitrarily large. By the definitions of the profiles, and (
6.5
) we obtain, for
J
large enough,

(B.2)

\left(w_{n}^{J}\left(s_{n}\right),\partial_{t}w_{n}^{J}\left(s_{n}\right)\right)\xrightharpoonup[n\to\infty]{}0\text{ in }\mathcal{H}.

As a consequence,
\lim_{n}\varepsilon_{n}=0
.

Case 2.
T_{j}=+\infty
.
Arguing as in Case 2 in the proof of (
6.10
), we obtain that there exists
g^{j}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
such that

\varepsilon_{n}=o_{n}(1)+\int_{\rho_{n}}^{\infty}g^{j}(r+t_{j,n}-s_{n})\Big(\partial_{t}w_{n}^{J}(s_{n},r)-\partial_{r}w_{n}^{J}(s_{n},r)\Big)r^{3/2}dr.

If
\lim_{n}\rho_{n}+t_{j,n}-s_{n}=+\infty
, we obtain right away that
\varepsilon_{n}=o_{n}(1)
.

If
\lim_{n}\rho_{n}+t_{j,n}-s_{n}=-\infty
, then

\int_{0}^{\rho_{n}}\left|g^{j}(r+t_{j,n}-s_{n})\right|^{2}dr\leq\int_{-\infty}^{\rho_{n}+t_{j,n}-s_{n}}|g(\eta)|^{2}\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}0,

and thus

\varepsilon_{n}=o_{n}(1)+\int_{0}^{\infty}g^{j}(r+t_{j,n}-s_{n})\Big(\partial_{t}w_{n}^{J}(s_{n},r)-\partial_{r}w_{n}^{J}(s_{n},r)\Big)r^{3/2}dr\\
=o_{n}(1)+\int\nabla_{t,x}V^{j}_{L}(s_{n}-t_{j,n})\cdot\nabla_{t,x}w_{n}^{J}(s_{n},x)dx,

where
V^{j}_{L}
is the radial solution of the linear wave equation such that

\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}|r^{3/2}\partial_{t}V^{j}_{L}(t,r)-g^{j}(r-t)|^{2}dr+\int_{0}^{\infty}|r^{3/2}\partial_{r}V^{j}_{L}(t,r)+g^{j}(r-t)|^{2}dr=0.

By conservation of the energy for the free wave equation (
3.1
),

\varepsilon_{n}=o_{n}(1)+\int\nabla_{t,x}V^{j}_{L}(0)\cdot\nabla_{t,x}w_{n}^{J}(t_{j,n},x)dx,

and thus by (
6.5
),
\varepsilon_{n}=o_{n}(1)
.

Finally, if
\lim_{n}\rho_{n}+t_{j,n}-s_{n}=c\in\mathbb{R}
, we see that

\varepsilon_{n}=o_{n}(1)+\int_{0}^{\infty}g^{j}_{c}(r+t_{j,n}-s_{n})\Big(\partial_{t}w_{n}^{J}(s_{n},r)-\partial_{r}w_{n}^{J}(s_{n},r)\Big)r^{3/2}dr\\
=o_{n}(1)+\int\nabla_{t,x}V^{j}_{L}(s_{n}-t_{j,n})\cdot\nabla_{t,x}w_{n}^{J}(s_{n},x)dx,

where
g_{c}(\eta)=1\!\!1_{\eta>c}g(\eta)
, and
V^{j}_{L}
is the radial solution of the linear wave equation such that

\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}|r^{3/2}\partial_{t}V^{j}_{L}(t,r)-g^{j}_{c}(r-t)|^{2}dr+\int_{0}^{\infty}|r^{3/2}\partial_{r}V^{j}_{L}(t,r)+g^{j}_{c}(r-t)|^{2}dr=0.

The same proof as before yields
\varepsilon_{n}=o_{n}(1)
.

Since the case
T_{j}=-\infty
can be treated exactly in the same way, the proof of (
6.11
) is complete.

B.2.
Pythagorean expansion of the energy for wave maps

In this subsection we prove (
7.21
). In view of Claim
7.2
, it is sufficient to prove

(B.3)

\displaystyle j\neq k\Longrightarrow\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\partial_{r}\Psi_{0,n}^{j}\partial_{r}\Psi_{0,n}^{k}rdr+\int_{0}^{\infty}\Psi_{1,n}^{j}\Psi_{1,n}^{k}rdr=0

(B.4)

\displaystyle j\neq k\Longrightarrow\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|\sin\Psi_{0,n}^{j}\sin\Psi_{0,n}^{k}\right|\frac{dr}{r}=0

(B.5)

\displaystyle j\leq J\Longrightarrow\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\partial_{r}\Psi_{0,n}^{j}\partial_{r}\omega_{0,n}^{J}rdr+\int_{0}^{\infty}\Psi_{1,n}^{j}\omega_{1,n}^{J}rdr=0

(B.6)

\displaystyle j\leq J\Longrightarrow\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|\sin\Psi_{0,n}^{j}\sin\omega_{0,n}^{J}\right|\frac{dr}{r}=0.

Recall that if
j\in\mathcal{J}_{L}
, i.e.
\lim_{n\to\infty}-t_{j,n}/\lambda_{j,n}\in\{\pm\infty\}
, then

\left(\Psi^{j}_{0,n}(r),\Psi^{j}_{1,n}(r)\right)=\left(\Psi^{j}_{L}\left(\frac{-t_{j,n}}{\lambda_{j,n}},\frac{r}{\lambda_{j,n}}\right),\frac{1}{\lambda_{j,n}}\partial_{t}\Psi_{L}^{j}\left(\frac{-t_{j,n}}{\lambda_{j,n}},\frac{r}{\lambda_{j,n}}\right)\right),

where
\Psi^{j}_{L}
is a solution of the linear equation (
7.6
) with initial data in
\mathbf{H}
. Thus
U^{j}=\frac{1}{r}\Psi^{j}
is a radial solution of the
1+4
dimensional wave equation. By standard dispersive estimates for linear wave equations,

(B.7)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|\Psi_{0,n}^{j}(r)\right|^{2}\frac{dr}{r}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|U^{j}\left(\frac{-t_{j,n}}{\lambda_{j,n}},r\right)\right|^{2}rdr=0.

This yields (
B.4
) when
j\in\mathcal{J}_{L}
or
k\in\mathcal{J}_{k}
, and (
B.6
) when
j\in\mathcal{J}_{L}
.

Next, we assume
j\in\mathcal{J}_{L}
and
k\in\mathcal{J}_{L}
and see that by (
B.7
) letting
U^{j}_{n}(t,r)=\frac{1}{\lambda_{j,n}}U^{j}\left(\frac{t-t_{j,n}}{\lambda_{j,n}},\frac{r}{\lambda_{j,n}}\right)
and defining similarly
U^{k}_{n}
, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\partial_{r}\Psi_{0,n}^{j}\partial_{r}\Psi_{0,n}^{k}rdr+\int_{0}^{\infty}\Psi_{1,n}^{j}\Psi_{1,n}^{k}rdr\\
=\int_{0}^{\infty}\partial_{r}U_{n}^{j}(0,r)\partial_{t}U_{n}^{j}(0,r)r^{3}dr+\int_{0}^{\infty}\partial_{r}U_{n}^{j}(0,r)\partial_{r}U_{n}^{j}(0,r)r^{3}dr+o_{n}(1),

and (
B.3
) follows from (
6.10
).

It remains to prove (
B.3
) when
j\in\mathcal{J}_{C}
or
k\in\mathcal{J}_{C}
, (
B.4
) when
j\in\mathcal{J}_{C}
and
k\in\mathcal{J}_{C}
, and (
B.5
), (
B.6
) when
j\in\mathcal{J}_{C}
.

Proof of (
B.3
) when
j\in\mathcal{J}_{C}
,
k\in\mathcal{J}_{L}
.
Assume to fix ideas
\lim_{n\to\infty}-t_{j,n}/\lambda_{j,n}=+\infty
. By the asymptotic formulas (
A.2
) and (
A.3
), there exists
G^{k}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
such that

\lim_{t\to+\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|r^{3/2}\partial_{t}U^{k}(t,r)+G^{k}(r-t)\right|dr+\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|r^{3/2}\partial_{r}U^{k}(t,r)-G^{k}(r-t)\right|dr.

Using also that
\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}|U^{k}(t,r)|^{2}rdr=0
, we deduce

\lim_{t\to+\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|r^{1/2}\partial_{t}\Psi^{k}(t,r)+G^{k}(r-t)\right|dr+\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|r^{1/2}\partial_{r}\Psi^{k}(t,r)-G^{k}(r-t)\right|dr.

Thus

\left|\int_{0}^{\infty}\partial_{r}\Psi_{0,n}^{j}(r)\partial_{r}\Psi_{0,n}^{k}(r)rdr\right|\\
=\left|\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\lambda_{j,n}}\partial_{r}\Psi_{0}^{j}\left(\frac{r}{\lambda_{j,n}}\right)G^{k}\left(\frac{t_{k,n}+r}{\lambda_{k,n}}\right)\frac{r^{1/2}}{\lambda_{k,n}^{1/2}}dr\right|\\
=\left|\int_{0}^{\infty}\partial_{r}\Psi_{0}^{j}\left(s\right)\frac{\lambda_{k,n}^{1/2}}{\lambda_{j,n}^{1/2}}G^{k}\left(\frac{t_{k,n}+\lambda_{j,n}s}{\lambda_{k,n}}\right)s^{1/2}ds\right|,

which goes to
0
when
n
goes to infinity (this is immediate when
G^{k}
is continuous and compactly supported, and follows by density in the general case). By a very similar computation,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Psi_{1,n}^{j}(r)\Psi_{1,n}^{k}(r)rdr=0,

which concludes the proof of (
B.3
) in this case.

Proof of (
B.3
) and (
B.4
) when
j,k\in\mathcal{J}_{C}
.

We have

\int_{0}^{\infty}\partial_{r}\Psi_{0,n}^{j}\partial_{r}\Psi_{0,n}^{k}rdr=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\lambda_{j,n}}\partial_{r}\Psi_{0}^{j}\left(\frac{r}{\lambda_{j,n}}\right)\frac{1}{\lambda_{k,n}}\partial_{r}\Psi_{0}^{k}\left(\frac{r}{\lambda_{k,n}}\right)rdr\\
=\int_{0}^{\mu_{n}}\ldots+\int_{\mu_{n}}^{\infty}\ldots,

where
\mu_{n}=\sqrt{\lambda_{j,n}\lambda_{k,n}}
. Using Cauchy-Schwarz on each of the integrals, we see that it goes to
0
when
n
goes to
\infty
, since
\lim_{n}\frac{\lambda_{j,n}}{\lambda_{k,n}}+\frac{\lambda_{k,n}}{\lambda_{j,n}}=\infty
. By the same proof for the term involving
\Psi_{1,n}^{j}
and
\Psi_{1,n}^{k}
, we obtain (
B.3
).

The proof of (
B.4
) when
j,k\in\mathcal{J}_{C}
is the same.

Proof of (
B.6
) when
j\in\mathcal{J}_{C}
.

We have

\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|\sin\Psi_{0,n}^{j}(r)\sin\omega_{0,n}^{J}(r)\right|\frac{dr}{r}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|\sin\Psi_{0}^{j}\left(r\right)\sin\omega_{0,n}^{J}(\lambda_{j,n}r)\right|\frac{dr}{r}.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. Then

\int_{\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon^{-1}}\left|\sin\Psi_{0}^{j}(r)\sin\omega_{0,n}^{J}(\lambda_{j,n}r)\right|\frac{dr}{r}\\
\leq\left(\int_{\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon^{-1}}\left|\sin\Psi_{0}^{j}(r)\right|^{3/2}\frac{dr}{r^{3/2}}\right)^{2/3}\left(\int_{\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon^{-1}}\left|\omega_{0,n}^{J}(\lambda_{j,n}r)\right|^{3}dr\right)^{1/3}.

Let
w_{n}^{J}(r)=\frac{1}{r}\omega_{n}^{J}(\lambda_{j,n}r)
. Then (
7.23
) implies that
\lambda_{j,n}w_{n}^{J}(\lambda_{j,n}\cdot)
, considered as a radial function on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
, converges weakly to
0
in
\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
. This implies that
\lambda_{j,n}w_{n}^{J}(\lambda_{j,n}\cdot)
converges strongly in
L^{3}_{\loc}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
, and thus

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon^{-1}}\left|\omega_{0,n}^{J}(\lambda_{j,n}r)\right|^{3}dr=0.

As a consequence, for all
\varepsilon

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon^{-1}}\left|\sin\Psi_{0}^{j}(r)\sin\omega_{0,n}^{J}(\lambda_{j,n}r)\right|\frac{dr}{r}=0,

and (
B.6
) follows since
\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin^{2}\Psi_{0}^{j}(r)\frac{dr}{r}
is finite and
\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin^{2}\omega_{0,n}^{J}(\lambda_{j,n}r)\frac{dr}{r}
is uniformly bounded.

Appendix C
Boundedness of integral operators on
L^{p}L^{q}
spaces

In this appendix, we consider, as in the core of the article, functions defined for
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
that are radial in the space variable. As before we use the notation

\|u\|_{(L^{p}L^{q})(R)}:=\left\|1\!\!1_{\{|x|>R+|t|\}}u\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R},L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{4}))}.

Lemma C.1
.

Let
R\geq 0
.
For
w\in(L^{2}L^{8})(R)
, define

(Aw)(t,r)=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\int_{0}^{t}w(\tau,r)d\tau.

Then
Aw\in(L^{2}L^{8/3})(R)
and

\left\|Aw\right\|_{(L^{2}L^{8/3})(R)}\lesssim\|w\|_{(L^{2}L^{8})(R)},

where the implicit constant is independent of
R\geq 0
.

Proof.

Step 1.
We first claim that if
h\in(L^{2}L^{8})(R)
, then
\frac{|t|}{r^{2}}h\in(L^{2}L^{8/3})(R)
. Indeed

\left(\int_{R+|t|}^{\infty}|h(t,r)|^{8/3}\left(\frac{|t|}{r^{2}}\right)^{8/3}r^{3}dr\right)^{3/8}\\
\lesssim|t|\left(\int_{R+|t|}h(t,r)^{8}r^{3}dr\right)^{1/8}\left(\int_{R+|t|}^{\infty}\frac{r^{3}}{r^{8}}dr\right)^{1/4}\\
\lesssim\frac{|t|}{R+|t|}\left(\int_{R+|t|}^{\infty}h(t,r)^{8}r^{3}dr\right)^{1/8}.

Now
\frac{|t|}{R+|t|}\leq 1
and
t\mapsto\left(\int_{R+|t|}^{\infty}h(t,r)^{8}r^{3}dr\right)^{1/8}
is in
L^{2}
in time, concluding this step.

Step 2.
In this step, we consider a positive function
f
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R},L^{8}(\mathbb{R}^{4}))
, and define

M(f)(t,x)=\sup_{I\ni t}\frac{1}{|I|}\int_{I}f(\tau,x)d\tau,

where the supremum is taken over all finite intervals
I
that contain
t
, and we prove

(C.1)

\left\|M(f)\right\|_{L^{2}L^{8}}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}L^{8}}.

Indeed, if
f\in L^{2}L^{2}
then

(C.2)

\|M(f)\|_{L^{2}L^{2}}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}L^{2}}

by Fubini and Hardy-Littlewood maximal theorem in the time variable. Furthermore, if
f\in L^{2}L^{\infty}
, one can prove:

(C.3)

\|M(f)\|_{L^{2}L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}L^{\infty}}.

Indeed,

M(f)(t,x)\lesssim\sup_{I\ni t}\frac{1}{|I|}\int_{I}\|f(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}d\tau

and thus

\|Mf(t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq\sup_{I\ni t}\frac{1}{|I|}\int_{I}\|f(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}d\tau.

Using Hardy-Littlewood maximum theorem in the
t
variable, we obtain (
C.3
). Interpolation gives (
C.1
).

Step 3.
Let
w\in(L^{2}L^{8})(R)
. Let

f(t,r)=|w(t,r)|1\!\!1_{\{r>t+R\}}.

Then, for
r>|t|+R
, we have

\frac{1}{|t|}\left|\int_{0}^{t}w(\tau,r)d\tau\right|\leq\frac{1}{|t|}\int_{-|t|}^{|t|}1\!\!1_{r>\tau+R}|w(\tau,r)|d\tau\leq 2M(f)(t,r),

since
r>|t|+R\geq|\tau|+R
for
0\leq|\tau|\leq|t|
. As a consequence, for
r>|t|+R
,

\left|A(w)(t,r)\right|\lesssim\frac{|t|}{r^{2}}M(f)(t,r).

By Step 1 with
h(t,r)=M(f)(t,r)
, then Step 2, we have

\left\|A(w)\right\|_{(L^{2}L^{8/3})(R)}\lesssim\|M(f)\|_{(L^{2}L^{8})(R)}\lesssim\|w\|_{(L^{2}L^{8})(R)}.

∎

Lemma C.2
.

Let
R\geq 1
,
\lambda>1/R
. For
w\in(L^{3}L^{6})(R)
, we define

(Bw)(t,r)=\frac{t}{r^{4}\log(r\lambda)^{1/2}}\int_{0}^{t}w(\tau,r)d\tau.

Then
B
is bounded from
(L^{3}L^{6})(R)
to
(L^{1}L^{2})(R)
and

\|Bw\|_{(L^{1}L^{2})(R)}\lesssim\frac{1}{\log(\lambda R)^{1/3}}\|w\|_{(L^{3}L^{6})(R)}

Proof.

We just work with
t>0
. By Minkowski in time,

\left(\int_{R+t}^{\infty}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\frac{w(\tau,r)t}{r^{4}\log(\lambda r)}d\tau\right)^{2}r^{3}dr\right)^{1/2}\leq\int_{0}^{t}\left\|w(\tau,r)\frac{t}{r^{4}\log(\lambda r)}\right\|_{L^{2}(R+t)}d\tau.

Furthermore, by Hölder

\left\|w(\tau,r)\frac{t}{r^{4}\log(\lambda r)}\right\|_{L^{2}(R+t)}=t\left(\int_{R+t}^{\infty}|w(\tau,r)|^{2}\frac{r^{3}}{r^{8}\log^{2}(\lambda r)}dr\right)^{1/2}\\
\leq t\left(\int_{R+t}^{\infty}|w(\tau,r)|^{6}r^{3}dr\right)^{1/6}\left(\int_{R+t}^{\infty}\frac{r^{3}}{r^{12}\log^{3}(\lambda r)}dr\right)^{1/3}\\
\lesssim\frac{t}{(R+t)^{8/3}\log(\lambda(R+t))}\int_{0}^{t}\left(\int_{R+t}^{\infty}|w(\tau,r)|^{6}r^{3}dr\right)^{1/6}d\tau

Integrating, we obtain

\left\|1\!\!1_{t>0}B(w)\right\|_{(L^{1}L^{2})(R)}\\
\lesssim\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{t}{(R+t)^{8/3}\log(\lambda(R+t))}\int_{0}^{t}\left(\int_{R+t}^{\infty}|w(\tau,r)|^{6}r^{3}dr\right)^{1/6}d\tau dt

By Fubini,

\left\|1\!\!1_{t>0}B(w)\right\|_{(L^{1}L^{2})(R)}\\
\lesssim\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{R+\tau}^{\infty}|w(\tau,r)|^{6}r^{3}dr\right)^{1/6}\int_{\tau}^{\infty}\frac{t}{(R+t)^{8/3}\log(\lambda(R+t))}dt\,d\tau\\
\lesssim\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{R+\tau}^{\infty}|w(\tau,r)|^{6}r^{3}dr\right)^{1/6}\int_{\tau}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{(R+t)^{5/3}\log(\lambda(R+t))}d\tau\\
\lesssim\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{R+\tau}^{\infty}|w(\tau,r)|^{6}r^{3}dr\right)^{1/6}\frac{d\tau}{(R+\tau)^{2/3}\log(\lambda(R+\tau))}\\
\lesssim\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{R+\tau}^{\infty}|w(\tau,r)|^{6}r^{3}dr\right)^{1/2}d\tau\right)^{1/3}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\tau}{(R+\tau)\log(\lambda(R+\tau))^{3/2}}d\tau\right)^{2/3},

where at the last line we have used Hölder’s inequality. The desired conclusion follows easily.
∎
